Hong Kong: Fire Anniversary Underscores Growing Repression

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Click to expand Image A memorial near the site of the deadly fire at the residential complex at Wang Fuk Court in Tai Po, Hong Kong, December 2, 2025. © 2025 Ng Han Guan/AP Photo

(London) – The Hong Kong government should ensure meaningful accountability for the devastating fire that killed 168 people one year ago and end its suppression of peaceful criticism, Human Rights Watch said today.

On November 26, 2025, Hong Kong’s deadliest fire in decades engulfed seven residential high-rise buildings at Wang Fuk Court, Tai Po. The authorities initiated an independent investigation and arrested three dozen people implicated in crimes. The Housing Minister resigned. But the authorities have also threatened and arbitrarily arrested those engaging in criticism and activism concerning the fire.

“The Hong Kong government responded to the horrific Wang Fuk Court fire by using Beijing’s tactics of shutting out the voices of the victims and promising an elusive accountability,” said Elaine Pearson, Asia director at Human Rights Watch. “The authorities need to recognize that the only way to prevent similar tragedies in the future is to allow people to speak out safely and hold those responsible fully accountable.”

The authorities have engaged in apparent acts of repression of free expression in the weeks ahead of the anniversary:

The media reported in November that the police made inquiries with Catholic churches planning masses to commemorate victims of the fire.A church in Tai Po subsequently removed references to the fire from its announcement and instead described the service as a mass for “the brothers and sisters who died in the past year;” hundreds nevertheless attended.A priest at another Catholic church posted on Facebook that three police officers had inquired about a planned memorial mass marking the anniversary, even though the church had not publicly announced the service.On November 22, a group working with migrant workers who survived the fire cancelled a book launch and drama event scheduled ahead of the anniversary, citing “unforeseen circumstances.” On November 23, the online news platform Boom News said it would remove some videos featuring interviews with survivors, quoting the whistleblower Edward Snowden on “censorship and fear.” The Boom News founder, Tang Ho-wing, had been arrested in September for alleged “sedition” for reporting on another politically sensitive anniversary.

Police Commissioner Joe Chow said police routinely asked organizers about upcoming public events, while decisions on whether to proceed “rested with the organizers themselves.”

Amid continued restrictions on freedom of expression and peaceful assembly in Hong Kong, Hong Kong communities around the world have planned commemorations of the fire.

Deep concerns surrounding the 2026 anniversary follow a year-long government crackdown on criticism, reporting, and civil society-organizing related to the fire. Immediately following the fire, hundreds of people gathered near Wang Fuk Court to donate and distribute supplies to victims, but as the volunteer effort grew, police told them to leave and government workers took over the site.

Authorities targeted people calling for accountability. Just four days after the fire, national security police reportedly arrested a university student, Miles Kwan, for “seditious intent” after he initiated an online petition calling for an independent investigation into the fire, and two others on suspicion of “inciting hatred against the government.”

Hong Kong and Beijing authorities subsequently accused “foreign forces” and “anti-China destabilizing forces” of using the fire to “incite hatred against the government.” Within hours of these announcements, two prominent online commentators, Ellie Yuen and Hailey Cheng, who had been frequently quoted in foreign media, said they would stop reporting on the fire.

National security police also arrested the online commentator Wong On-yin in December after he planned to hold a news conference about the Wang Fuk Court fire and discussed on YouTube his subsequent questioning by police. The authorities prosecuted him for allegedly obstructing a national security investigation and publishing “seditious material.” He remains in custody awaiting a verdict in January 2027.

Between April and May 2026, the authorities barred Wang Fuk Court residents from displaying banners from their homes or being accompanied by journalists as they were briefly permitted to return to their apartments to retrieve belongings.

Also in May, the authorities arrested the former Wang Fuk Court owners’ committee member Kong Cheung-fat and his wife on allegations of fraud and money laundering. Kong had joined other owners in seeking a meeting to discuss insurance and other post-fire issues. While out on bail, he said he would no longer give interviews or comment publicly. Some residents said his arrest made them more cautious about speaking publicly.

An independent committee, set up by Hong Kong Chief Executive John Lee, published a report on October 29 that found wide-ranging regulatory failures across four government bodies, including inadequate oversight of renovation work and fire safety, weaknesses in building maintenance regulation, and supervisory failures by contractors and other professionals. That day, Lee and senior officials formally apologized to the victims of the fire and their families while promising to implement “systemic reforms.” Housing Secretary Winnie Ho later stepped down from office while the authorities arrested 35 people in connection with the fire and possible corruption in the renovation project.

“An apology and promises of reform are no substitute for ensuring that people can freely question and criticize the authorities,” Pearson said. “The Hong Kong government should act on the independent committee’s recommendations and provide adequate compensation and other support for victims and their families.”

Chris Evans And The Rock’s Overlooked Christmas Movie With An 89% Audience Score Is Streaming On Prime Video

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Chris Evans and Dwayne “The Rock” Johnson are two of the most famous actors working today. As such, both have been in seemingly countless huge projects, from the Avengers movies for Evans to the Fast and Furious franchise for The Rock. Because of this, both actors have appeared in movies that, comparatively speaking, have fallen through the cracks.

That’s certainly the case for Red One, the Jake Kasdan-directed action Christmas film from Amazon, which is currently available for streaming on Prime Video. Red One, which was written by Chris Morgan (who penned Fast and Furious: Tokyo Drift), tells the story of the North Pole’s head of security as he teams up with a well-respected hacker in order to find Santa Claus before Christmas Eve, as he’s been kidnapped.

Red One wasn’t a huge hit with critics, earning a 29% score on Rotten Tomatoes. The film also failed to earn back its production budget, taking in just over $180 million off a $250 million cost. Despite that, Red One proved to be much more popular with fans, as the film has an 89% audience score on Rotten Tomatoes and consistently sits near the top of the charts on Prime Video each holiday season.

As the holidays are quickly approaching, many are looking to streaming services to watch a cozy movie this season. There are, of course, the certified Christmas classics, such as Home Alone, A Christmas Story, and How the Grinch Stole Christmas, but movies like Red One are also deserving of a watch around this time of year, especially if you’re looking for something new and fresh this time around.

As stated, Red One follows the North Pole head of security, Callum Drift, who’s played by The Rock. Drift is a loyal servant to Santa Claus, who’s portrayed by JK Simmons, and the two have worked together for hundreds of years. Red One tells the story of how that centuries-long relationship nearly falls apart due to an evil scheme to capture Father Christmas just before his busiest day of the year.

This naturally sends the entirety of the North Pole into a panic. With Santa Claus gone, it becomes a real possibility that there will be no Christmas at all, leaving millions of children all around the world without gifts under their tree. Drift, however, is determined not to let that happen, and is willing to do whatever it takes to bring Santa Claus back as quickly as possible.

Through the help of Mythological Oversight and Restoration Authority (MORA), an organization that helps to keep the peace between human and mythological societies, Drift is able to determine that the kidnappers discovered Santa’s location through the work of an infamous hacker named Jack O’Malley, played by Chris Evans.

Despite his involvement, O’Malley had no idea what he was hired to do, and instead just completed the task as he would with any other job. Drift then recruits O’Malley, who has long since been a skeptic of the existence of Santa Claus, to help bring back Simmons’ character, with time quickly running down.

Many aspects of Red One were criticized by critics, but The Rock and Chris Evans both give strong performances in their respective roles. As such, there are still reasons to watch or rewatch it now on Prime Video.

One of the most criticized aspects of Red One was the clear franchise set up that the film integrated throughout. An entire world was being established, showing not just the North Pole, but the South Pole (with Krampus himself) and other mythological locations and figures as well. This bothered some, who said that it made Red One feel too much like a clear attempt to build a Christmas-themed cinematic universe rather than simply making a good movie.

However, on the other hand, a lot of Red One’s world building actually does work. The presence of Krampus, for example, helps to make the world feel truly lived in, meaning that it brought more to the movie than just an attempt at future franchise success. And, of course, Red One did have plenty of franchise potential, but it doesn’t look as if that will ever come to fruition. Despite that, audiences can still enjoy Red One on Prime Video this holiday season.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Illustrations of Moral Dissonance

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 25, 2026 | Reviewed by Lybi Ma

The Trojan War was in its tenth year. A soothsayer had foretold the Greeks that they would win the war only by retrieving the famed archer, Philoctetes, whose bow was from Heracles and whose arrows never missed their mark. The major problem, though, was that Odysseus and his men had abandoned Philoctetes on their way to Troy those many years earlier. On a stop before they reached Troy, Philoctetes had inadvertently wandered into a sacred area and been bitten by a snake. He developed a wound so suppurative and foul-smelling and cried out in such agony that no one could stand to be with him, and Odysseus ordered his shipmates to drop him off on the deserted island of Lemnos (Sophocles; Heaney, 1961).

For all that time, Philoctetes, miserable and alone, suffered with a virulent, gangrenous, and oozing wound that did not heal, "an insatiable sickness" (Sophocles). All the while, he nursed a grudge against Odysseus and his men, and he was obsessed with the betrayal (Lansky, 2003; Wilson, 2018).

There are passing references to this myth in both Homer's Iliad and Odyssey, as well as in Ovid's Metamorphoses and Virgil's Aeneid, but the main extant source is Sophocles’ play, Philoctetes. The story has also been immortalized in many works of art.

Odysseus — the clever manipulator and far more devious than depicted in Christopher Nolan's recent film, The Odyssey — devised an elaborate plan: He and some of his men would land on Lemnos with Neoptolemus, the young son of Achilles, and trick Philoctetes into coming, with his magic bow, back with them to Troy.

Commanded by Odysseus and threatened with punishment for treason if he would not comply, Neoptolemus was to befriend Philoctetes and explain that he, too, had a grievance against Odysseus. He had reason, therefore, to abandon the Greeks and take Philoctetes home, all the while plotting to bring him to Troy.

But Neoptolemus was a young man of character: "It is not in my nature to practice treachery...Don't you think it shameful to lie?" he asks. "It goes against all I was ever brought up to believe," he adds. (Heaney). "Not if the lie brings deliverance...Troy can be taken only with his bow," responds Odysseus.

Neoptolemus ultimately agrees to accept his commander's duplicitous plan that includes the possibility of stealing the bow and leaving Philoctetes to starve.

Seeing the extraordinary anguish of Philoctetes moves Neoptolemus, as he says, "My heart is bursting with grief at your pain." With renewed courage and honor, he refuses to acquiesce to Odysseus' deception (Segal, 1995). "What is disgusting is when a man abandons his own true nature and acts shamefully," says Neoptolemus.

Though Neoptolemus confesses his real intentions to Philoctetes, it will take a deus ex machina in the form of the appearance of Heracles to convince Philoctetes that his destiny is to accompany Odysseus, Neoptolemus, and the men to Troy. Philoctetes, as foretold, will have his wound healed and will be "hailed as the bravest of all the Greeks" with the fall of Troy: Philoctetes will kill Paris, and Neoptolemus will kill King Priam (Wilson).

Sophocles’ play depicts conflicts between the individual and society (Segal, 1995) and poignantly illustrates the effects of chronic, unremitting disease for which someone is ostracized and abandoned. Most significantly, it reflects the high moral cost of compromising one's integrity (Rangell, 1974), as Neoptolemus had, and experiencing moral dissonance (ter Heide and Olff, 2023; VanderWeele et al, 2025), or moral injury (Shay, 1991; 1994; 2002; 2011; Griffin et al, 2019; Litz, 2025; Litz and Walker, 2025).

Psychoanalytically, this compromise of integrity results from a conflict between the ego, the rational part of the brain, and the superego, one's conscience (Rangell, 1974).

Jonathan Shay is credited with introducing the term moral injury in the context of the military. It is a betrayal of what’s right and a “shrinkage of the moral and social horizon.” Moral hypocrisy occurs when immoral actions occur in otherwise moral people (Naso, 2006).

Shay compared Homeric soldiers with American soldiers: Homeric soldiers respected, even honored their enemy, without derogatory name-calling or dehumanizing them, as was commonly encouraged by some in the American armed forces.

Shay thought of moral injury as psychological wound contamination, a kind of leadership malpractice. There are three parts to his definition: there is a betrayal of what’s right, a high-stakes situation, and someone who holds legitimate authority over a person (2011). Moral distress results from "culturally imbued shared and internalized values" (Griffin et al.).

Others have extended the use of the term to situations beyond the military (Litz, 2025), though there is no consensus definition or gold standard of measurement (Litz and Walker, 2025). Further, research suffers from small sample sizes and even disagreement about what is a potentially morally injurious event (Griffin et al., 2019).

“The human story is replete with individuals, groups, and nations wittingly and unwittingly perpetrating cruel and pernicious acts of inhumanity against others." Psychological consequences ensue. “People can be consumed and haunted by these inhumane acts” (Litz and Walker). Moral injury now includes acts of commission or omission, being the victim of, or bearing witness to acts “that transgress deeply held moral beliefs and expectations” (Litz and Walker).

Situations that can evoke moral injury include the My Lai Massacre in March 1968, where American soldiers slaughtered at least 300 unarmed Vietnamese civilians (Borch, 2018).

The torture and abuse of prisoners by Americans at Abu Ghraib in Iraq in 2003 is another example.

Though the effects of moral injury can overlap with PTSD, the two are distinct. PTSD is often fear-based and involves flashbacks, exaggerated startle reflex, memory loss, nightmares, and insomnia (Griffin et al.).

The syndrome of moral injury, in distinction, does not require a life-threatening event (Litz, 2024). It is "persistent moral distress" (VanderWeel et al.), more likely involving feelings of guilt, shame, anger, anhedonia, social isolation (Griffin et al.), loss of trust in others or institutions, failure to forgive oneself or others, and loss of meaning in life (VanderWeel et al.).

The damage from moral injury can be irreparable and exceptionally difficult to treat. Cognitive-behavioral coping approaches, such as habituation and reappraisal, often fail to relieve the distress (Griffin et al.).

Borch FL. 2018. What really happened on 16 March 1968? What lessons have been learned? A look at the My Lai incident fifty years later. https://armyhistory.org/my-lai/. (retrieved 11/21/26).

How To Complete The Final Flush In GTA 6

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

You can get a free stamina upgrade in GTA 6 by helping out a conspiracy theorist, but there's a dark easter egg to uncover after you're done, too.

I'm convinced that Lucia is a clone in Grand Theft Auto 6 — at least, in my playthrough. If you happen on a little side quest called The Final Flush, where you help a conspiracy theorist uncover the secret machinations of Big Sewage, things get a little weird. Weird enough that yes, I really am convinced Lucia is a clone. Just don't let the FIB hear me posting about that on Snapmatic, yeah?

In all seriousness, this is one of those oddities in GTA 6 where you don't really understand what's happening, things veer a little X-Files, and then you're handed a permanent stamina upgrade for your troubles as though you haven't been wading through literal crap.

Like all good encounters, you start The Final Flush by scanning a QR code on a handwritten poster by a toilet that screams "STOP! DON'T FLUSH." I also found the same poster in the storage compartment of a pawn shop, but if you want a reliable way to begin the mission instead of going on a Vice City crime spree until you happen across one, head to the north of Corsair Key in Mariana County and enter the only building on the entire island - the toilet.

Use the WaiNK IT app to scan the QR code (#NOFLUSHERS) and you'll get a message asking you to reply "TRUTH" to join the movement. If you decide to embrace your inner tinfoil hat, you'll get a follow-up message telling you to meet the sender at Frank's Waffles in Vice City, whereupon arriving you'll need to repeat the code: 00-00-02.

Drive to Frank's Waffles, and you'll see an older man muttering to himself at a booth by the toilets. This is the colorful Dr. Merle, a conspiracy theorist with an ingenious plan: swallow a tracker, poop it out, and follow it to the source.

Of course, as Lucia rightfully points out (you can also do this mission with Jason), he could've just thrown the tracker in the toilet and saved himself the trouble. But he waffles about how Node is too smart for that, and that he needs to be stealthier.

If you return to Frank's Waffles and check the toilet later, well, I decided not to include the screenshot.

The point is, supposedly, Big Sewage is harvesting Leonidan DNA through their poop to make clones of its citizens, and Dr. Merle wants to get to the bottom of it.

Immediately after depositing the tracker, Dr. Merle will ask you to drive his van as he yells directions at you. You can take a guess at where the tracker in the sewage pipes leads — the sewage plant.

Fast and Furious fans have an extra special easter egg to find in GTA 6: Paul Walker's Mitsubishi Eclipse from the first film.

Just make sure you have the compass enabled, as you won't get a trail on your mini-map to follow, only sporadic directions from Dr. Merle as he tells you to drive east, north, and in whatever direction the pipes are leading.

Eventually, you will get close enough that a waypoint appears on the beach. Park the van on the coast and follow Dr. Merle on foot along the fence, where he'll start lockpicking a gate to get you inside.

Dr. Merle will ask you to keep watch while he very slowly works a lock that Lucia and Jason could pick in seconds. You can't really do much though, because it's a chain-link fence that security can easily see through.

So, when a van pulls up asking what exactly you're doing, select 'Defuse' to tell the worker you're fixing the gate. They won't believe you, but it will buy you enough time to get inside and start sneaking about.

Once inside, head to the pink area marked on your map. You can either talk to the workers and lie that you're electricians, or sneak past them through the double doors into the adjacent building (like with all interiors, it's marked by a darker shade of blue).

Here, Dr. Merle will break down and ramble about how close he got to finding out the truth, only to be thwarted by concrete, as the tracker is right below your feet. This is where things take a turn. His poop-laden tracker isn't just lurking in underground pipes, there's a secret facility.

Head to the balcony overseeing the room where there's a control panel with three buttons. Turn all the lights on the panel green (press the bottom two) and then interact with the main lever. Right where Dr. Merle is sitting, a secret staircase will be revealed leading to a strange chamber.

Naturally, like with any conspiracy theorist worth his salt, Dr. Merle puts his idea to the test and climbs into the chamber. It quickly fills with a strange, viscous green liquid, and he asks you — Jason or Lucia — to press the button on the control panel and quite literally flush him. Do it.

One of GTA 6's best easter eggs comes with some very unique accessories. Here's how to get them.

The room blares with the sound of the agitated water authority warning you that the police are en route and that the chamber is toxic and should not be entered. But if you want a permanent stamina upgrade, I'm afraid you have to get inside and follow Dr. Merle's example.

You'll enter first-person while it fills with that strange green liquid again, and bizarrely, you wake up outside your safehouse, unclear how you got there, stamina upgrade in hand.

Okay, I'm not only convinced that my Lucia is a clone. I have proof. And this is where the guide takes a turn. After completing The Big Flush, you can find a disturbing little easter egg revealing what exactly happened in the chamber.

Head north to Lummox County and travel to the eastern island. Make sure you don't stumble onto Hyde Island, or you'll end up being hunted for sport.

Near the tip, there's a sealed bunker. Enter the code '3612' to open the door, and head on inside. There's no reward here, just a disturbing revelation that will change how you see the rest of the game.

Through the bunker and down a ladder, you'll see three corpses — so long as you've done the mission first: Dr. Merle, whoever you put in the chamber, and an unknown bald man (I checked his face, it isn't Jason).

Naturally, as you can see in the video embedded above, I brought Jason along so he could find out that the Lucia at home is really a clone, which makes every single text and interaction feel just a little bit off. But hey, a free stamina upgrade is a free stamina upgrade.

Or... maybe these are discarded clones. Who's to say?

Leonida's zoo is one of GTA 6's most impressive locations. Here's how to see it for yourself.

Ezra Frech Reacts to Prosthetic Falling Off Mid-Dance During DWTS

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Before Ezra Frech and Daniella Karagach were crowned the Dancing With the Stars season 35 winners, they seamlessly navigated Frech’s prosthetic leg falling off mid-dance.

There was a moment Ezra Frech didn’t have a leg to stand on—but he had a ballroom supporting him.

After the Paralympian’s prosthetic leg came off during his freestyle dance alongside partner Daniella Karagach on the Dancing With the Stars Nov. 24 finale, he revealed what went through his head before seamlessly reattaching the limb with the help of his partner as well as pro Alan Bersten.

“The leg just wasn't on correctly, and I felt it,” he told Variety following the episode, which saw him and Karagach crowned as the season’s winners. “Sometimes it'll readjust and I'll be okay. And so, as I was running up the steps, I'm like, ‘There's a 50 percent chance my leg will resuction and I'll be okay.’ And that wasn't the case.”

Nevertheless, he was ready to do what had to be done to finish the dance. After all, it was a moment he and Karagach had long been preparing for.

“I was just like, ‘Okay, I'm gonna do the rest of the dance on one leg. Like I'll do the entire thing on one leg,’” the 21-year-old continued. “She's drilled in me since the beginning that no matter what happens, we keep pushing through. You never give up, and you fight for it. And in this case, regardless of the circumstance, I was gonna do whatever I can to fight for it.”

Thanks to the quick thinking of his pro, however, Frech was able to get a leg back up the competition—literally. During a brief pause in the dance, Karagach appeared to yell something to her partner as she handed him his prosthetic, allowing Frech a few beats to reattach the limb.

“I heard her say, ‘Put your leg on,’” he recalled, “and I was like, ‘Yes, okay, yeah, good idea. I'll put it on right now.’”

While Karagach had the utmost faith in her partner’s ability to persevere, she knew  taking the time to reattach the limb was worth it.

“I was just like, there is no other place in the routine that he can do this right now, and he cannot do the whole routine on one leg,” she explained. “So, I was like, ‘Just put your leg on.’ And it's not like—we had one little trick in there that he did on one, like a 540. It was really impressive. It was really great. But nothing's better than him just clicking on his leg properly and continuing on with the dance. I was like, ‘This is our only shot to do that.’”

The moment was so adrenaline inducing, however, that Frech admitted he didn’t quite remember how his prosthetic made it back onto his body.

“Did somebody give me the leg? Because I blacked out,” he asked aloud, to which Karagach confirmed she did. “You gave me the leg? Really?”

Thankfully, those few seconds were the only thing Frech needed.

“I clicked it on,” he said, “and when I put it on the second time. I was like, ‘Ooh it's on now, we're good.’”

And good he was, with he and Karagach crowned the season 35 winners by the end of the episode—an accomplishment as good as gold for Frech.

"This is the most special moment of my life," he told the audience. "Thank you, everybody, for voting. Thank you for believing in us. Oh my God."

For a look back at the other dancing pairs Frech and Karagach had to outstep all the way to the Len Goodman Mirrorball Trophy, read on.

The 10 Things I Hate About You star had her last dance with her pro partner during Nov. 2's Disney Night.

The celebrity chef couldn't cha-cha-chop away the competition and was eliminated during the Sept. 22 episode.

What did you miss on Glee? The actor taking his musical talents to the dance floor.

Sadly, the former '90s teen heartthrob did not MMMBop his way to the top of the leaderboard on season 35. He and his pro partner were axed from the competition on Sept. 29.

The Fresh Prince of Bel-Air actress will be bringing her fresh moves to the dance floor.

Twenty years after starring in Step Up, the dancer stepped up—and out—when she got cut from the competition in a shocking Nov. 10 elimination.

Rachael Leigh Cook Talks Her New Hallmark Rom-Com, Parenting Teens & 'She's All That'

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Double Booked for the Holidays star (and mom of two) opens up about being in the parenting-a-teen trenches with the rest of us.

For a whole generation of us, Rachael Leigh Cook will forever be Laney Boggs walking down those stairs in *the* red dress. But these days, in addition to being a busy working actor, she’s also a mom of a 13-year-old and an 11-year-old, which means she’s living through the tween and teen years like the rest of us. (“Mixed job performance reviews from my 13-year-old, for sure,” she admits.) And, yep, her daughter has even seen Cook’s iconic ‘90s movies like She’s All That. Her review? She’s glad her mom’s eyebrows grew back.

Also like so many of us, Cook’s a total sucker for a cozy holiday rom-com. It just so happens she’s currently starring in one: Double Booked for the Holidays, now streaming on Hallmark+. She plays identical twins Abigail and Charlie, who head to Wales to a bookish Airbnb for their first Christmas after losing their mom.

When we caught up with Cook, she filled us in on playing double duty, the “say yes” theory that led her to her boyfriend, and the ‘90s icons she considers go-to comfort watches.

Scary Mommy: You're playing twins, which means you're your own scene partner. Which sister is more you, Abigail or Charlie?

Rachael Leigh Cook: I think our producers thought of Abigail as the sort of anchor character in the story. Her love story gets a little bit more screen time, but I just related a lot more to the Charlie character. So that sort of just felt a little easier to latch onto and to have fun with because I'm always just trying to have the best time. When you make these movies, I feel like the audience's enjoyment is my responsibility. If the scene is informational, I'm going to try to make it go fast for you. If it could be a little bit silly, I'm going to make it as silly as they let me. I want it to be fun for you. So when I got to be Charlie and I felt like I got to deliver a little bit more razzle dazzle or whatever you want to call it, that's what felt the best.

SM: What did you do to make the twins feel different? Because I literally had to keep reminding myself there are not, in fact, two of you.

RLC: Julie, that's the nicest thing that you could say. That's what I was utterly terrified of. I read the script, and I thought, Uh-oh, this is going to just seem like I'm talking to myself if I'm not careful because I'm very guilty of being a multi-screen viewer. I'm not afraid to look at my phone. Those shows where if someone just looks at someone and it means something and then you don't understand the next scene, I'm lost. I can't handle it. So I knew that for people like me who might also drift off and just get a snack, I needed everything to be clear. I couldn't have people dipping in and out and feeling confused. This is a holiday movie. We're not trying to confuse anyone.

I really just tried to separate vocally and work from the top of my vocal range to the basement. And that's a little bit difficult because I realized that at the top I can't project very well. But other than that, I feel like it worked pretty well. I just tried to separate the physicality as much as I could so that people weren't confused. We realized that just because of the quick schedule we're on, I couldn't even change hair between the two very much. I got concerned pretty early on that it was just going to be a giant melting pot of too much of me, and that seemed just real bad. So I thought I got to just separate them as much as I can. And if I'm bad, I'm bad, but no one's confused.

SM: You once said something I loved, which is that you’re happiest to be alive when you're living wholly unique experiences.

SM: Did Christmas in a Welsh bookshop movie count?

RLC: Absolutely. I didn't even read the whole script before I said yes. I probably shouldn't admit that, but I just thought no one's going to call and ask if I want to play my own twin ever again in Wales. I can figure out the rest. I'll be fine. This is a say yes moment if there ever was one.

SM: For sure. Any other recent examples that made that list?

RLC: It was I think a little over a year and a half ago, but I remember this day because I got two back-to-back emails. And, listen, most days as an actor are things like, Yeah, they don't want you for that part or Oh, here's a two-cent residual check or whatever. It ain't all glamor.

But one day I got two back-to-back emails and one of them said, "Do you want to be a guest on Name That Tune?" And the other one right after it said, "Do you want to be in a float in the Hollywood Christmas Parade?" And I just wrote, "Yes, send, yes, send." And before I knew it, I had two completely wild experiences because of those things. Because of one of them, I met my now boyfriend, and because of the other one, I got to go and play Name That Tune with Michelle Williams from Destiny's Child.

So that was a pretty awesome validation of my say yes theory.

SM: I am obsessed with everything you just said. Also, that sounds like its own little mini Hallmark movie — you met your boyfriend?! Come on.

RLC: I mean, Hallmark can do just about no wrong as far as I'm concerned. They provide so much cortisol-free entertainment for people with wildly stressful jobs and lives, and I'm all about it. I'm drinking that Kool-Aid all day.

SM: Me too, sister. Well, I want to dip into some nostalgia because we actually recently put your She's All That dress on our list of pop culture dresses we were all obsessed with growing up. You’ve said it didn’t really change you, but rather it revealed your confidence. Do you have a real-life article of clothing that does that for you now?

RLC: I almost don't want to draw attention to it because I know that you'll then see how many times I've worn this. I have this great tracksuit by... Oh my gosh, I can't remember. Why can't I even remember who makes it? I got it in New York. It's sort of cranberry colored. It has stripes on the sleeves. I can get this information to you. And it's matching pants and a top, and there's almost nowhere that this set can't go because you can wear it with heels and some big jewelry and it feels very sort of casual, upscale, athleisure, or you can wear it with sneakers and no extra anything and it just looks like you put some thought into it.

That is my I don't know what to wear outfit. Everyone should have one of those.

SM: Oh, 100%. Well, I know you're in the thick of the tween and early teen years. I have a 13-year-old and a 15-year-old, so we're in it. Number one: how are you?

RLC: Thank you for asking. I think I'm doing fine, but yeah, mixed job performance reviews from my 13-year-old, for sure. I sometimes just feel like I can do no right, and it's really difficult. I'm trying to take what my friends with older kids say about, Oh, it's just because they feel so safe with you that they can just live as their frustrated self, and then they go to school and they behave well and toe the line and all of that. So I'm just like, yep, I'm so honored, so honored. It's a bumpy ride over here, but it's also the very best, and I wouldn't change a thing about it.

I'm absolutely horrified because I can see almost the first break of dawn's light far in the distance of them wanting to leave my home, and that is totally unacceptable. I'm like, "No, you have to stay my babies forever." My son is 11 and still doesn't love sleeping with all the lights off, so I'm hopeful that I can feel like I'm still the mom to a young child in some small ways anyway.

SM: Like you said, it's very day-to-day, so we're just rolling with it. But it's also cool to get to see more of who they're becoming, which I think is really fun.

RLC: Very well said. That's the incredible part. Even though it's the worst thing ever when those memory photos show up on your phone of them as a baby — it's painful because you can't have that time anymore — it's like, "Well, yeah, but I also didn't know you as well."

SM: Exactly. Your daughter, Charlotte, is 13 now… has she watched She’s All That or Josie and the Pussycats yet?

RLC: She has. And they came with me to a screening of Josie at the Hollywood Forever Cemetery pretty recently, and there were, I think, 4,000 people there. It was a big group, and I feel really grateful to them and the revival houses because I don't know if in this day and age there's any chance that my kids would be able to experience work I've done on that scale ever again. I think the kids were mostly in it for the candy, but they were very respectful.

I think my daughter said she's glad my eyebrows grew back, and so am I.

SM: Kids… they sure know how to keep you humble! Last question: As a ‘90s and early ‘00s icon, you’re a comfort watch for a lot of us.

SM: I’m curious, what is your comfort watch?

RLC: Oh, man. I geeked out over... I met Elizabeth Shue one time. I can't tell you how many times I've seen Adventures in Babysitting. So someone like that. I've never met Molly Ringwald, but I would be the least cool human in the world. I grew up on John Hughes movies and her. So yeah, I'd say those.

SM: I love it. You’ve been a true joy, and it's such an honor to meet you.

RLC: Mine! And you're doing great. You really are.

SM: You too. We’ll get to the other side of all this; we will.

SM: Oh, babe, that’s a convo for a whole other interview.

This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity.

Why Blade Runner 2099 Skipped Over The Replicant Uprising [Exclusive]

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

"Welcome to a future we no longer control." So says the official tagline for the Prime Video series "Blade Runner 2099," which is set (self-evidently) 50 years after the events of Denis Villeneuve's "Blade Runner 2049" movie. The world has undergone some seriously seismic changes since then, as viewers will quickly realize in the opening moments of the show. Where both Ridley Scott's classic 1982 film "Blade Runner" and Villeneuve's 2017 sequel took place under very traditional confines (with humanity ruling over bioengineered Replicants as second-class citizens), "2099" has flipped the script entirely. Instead, the Replicants have orchestrated a full-scale uprising and seized control.

Not that we'll actually see any of that go down within "Blade Runner 2099," mind you. The series premiere opens well after this revolutionary turn of events has become old news. It's a bold and decisive creative choice, and one that I had to ask showrunner Silka Luisa about during a recent press junket. Why set this story in the aftermath of such a development rather than during it? As she tells it, it had to do with telling a story we haven't already seen in pop culture before:

"To be honest, I feel like I've seen that before. Not with Replicants, but definitely the robot revolution is definitely something I can close my eyes and picture. It's been in the zeitgeist and people have done different takes on it, so it didn't seem like I was going to be putting anything out into the world that people couldn't imagine for themselves."

Reading between the lines, it's true that properties like "Terminator" have already covered this ground before. And, in fairness, a wartime drama wouldn't quite mesh with the neo-noir vibes of "Blade Runner." But, for Luisa, her reasons extend even deeper than that.

Considering the timeline established in "Blade Runner" and "2049," the Replicant uprising in "Blade Runner 2099" has been almost a century in the making. The Denis Villeneuve sequel even took steps to directly integrate this possibility into its plot, keeping a Replicant rebellion simmering in the background of its story. In the Prime Video series, this powder keg has been detonated, and now the various characters (including Michelle Yeoh's Replicant Blade Runner Olwen and Hunter Schafer's incognito human Cora) have to live with the consequences.

According to Silka Luisa, this was a challenge that she embraced from the start. It's fairly easy to tell a straightforward story about revolution, but it's much harder to depict what comes next. As she told me over Zoom, "Whereas, what I really wasn't sure about myself at the beginning and that was interesting, was what happens afterwards. What does it look like to lose? And if you have been disempowered for your entire existence, what do you do with power?"

It's a fascinating question to pose to a group of sentient beings frequently described as "more human than human." They make look, walk, and bleed like humans ... but Replicants have always been designed to be far stronger, physically. When the oppressed throw off their shackles, do they then become the oppressors in turn? According to Luisa:

"Those were just, at least to me personally, much more interesting questions than just a simple idea of which side is gonna win. When, in truth, you kind of know which side is gonna win [laughs]."

All eight episodes of "Blade Runner 2099" are currently streaming on Prime Video.

Cyberpunk 2 Development Really Ramping Up as Team Targets Big 2027

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

2027 will likely be a pivotal year for Cyberpunk 2 as it rockets towards full production, with CD Projekt Red anticipating significant expansion to the sequel's development team.

Since the summer, the number of people working on Cyberpunk 2 has increased from 184 to 199, which doesn't seem like much, but this is a steady process.

By the end of next year, management expects there to be a total of around 320 devs — a team size that would be comparable to the 500-strong studio that's currently preparing The Witcher 4 for its 2028 release.

In other words, we can probably assume Cyberpunk 2 will leave early production in the relatively near future — and that's a big deal, because it means that the bones of the project are basically locked in place.

CDPR's Karolina Gnaś, who maintains relationships with the company's investors, reiterates that "good progress" is being made with Cyberpunk 2 — with further comments suggesting that there have been no internal delays.

The thing is, we're probably not going to see the Cyberpunk 2077 successor until The Witcher 4 is out, which seems a rather daunting prospect.

But it's worth remembering that CDPR is now essentially split between two major studios — one in Poland (The Witcher 4) and one in the US (Cyberpunk 2). The company's grown massively since The Witcher 3 first launched over a decade ago.

One Of The Most Successful VR Games Of All Time Is Coming To Xbox In January 2027

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Say hello to the flatscreen version of Arizona Sunshine

If you've ever owned any kind of VR headset in your life, you've probably come across a game known as Arizona Sunshine, and we're pleased to say that its new flatscreen version is coming to Xbox Series X and Series S in January!

This is a zombie apocalypse game that served as one of the most successful examples of a proper single-player FPS in the early VR era, first releasing all the way back in 2016. It's obviously received a lot of changes for the Xbox release, most notably in the form of a new third-person perspective rather than sticking with first-person.

Its story focuses around a survivor called "Sunny" and his dog known as "Buddy", the latter of which is now playable in two-player co-op as of this new release. Don't be fooled by Buddy's cuteness though - it's still a gory game and certainly not intended for kids, so just keep that in mind before you hit the purchase button or the trailer up above!

Anyway, Arizona Sunshine was a big deal 10 years ago, so it'll be fascinating to see how well it fares on modern non-VR platforms. The game is already available to pre-order on the Xbox Store at a low price of £21.99 / $24.99, and it also seemingly comes with Xbox Play Anywhere support (meaning you get a free PC copy too).

We'll leave you with more details on Arizona Sunshine below, launching for Xbox on January 21st of 2027.

Sharpen your machete and grab your friends. Arizona Sunshine® brings its award-winning zombie apocalypse to explosive third-person action, featuring online and split-screen co-op, brutal combat, dark humor, and Buddy, your loyal zombie-chomping canine companion.

Max Hurleman remains on Steelers' practice squad, available to other teams

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers’ swipe at the team’s front office about wide receiver Max Hurleman pointed out something all 31 other teams should be considering: There’s a talented, cheap receiver and special teams player available to be signed off the Steelers’ practice squad.

Hurleman got the first regular-season action of his career on Sunday against the Eagles and was just about the only bright spot in an otherwise dreary offensive performance for Pittsburgh. Hurleman was the Steelers’ leading receiver with six catches for 61 yards, and he scored the Steelers’ only touchdown. Hurleman also played 10 special teams snaps and returned three punts for 36 yards and two kickoffs for 32 yards.

Rodgers said Hurleman should have been on the Steelers’ 53-player roster all along, but instead he has been on the practice squad all season, and reverted back to the practice squad after Sunday’s game. Rodgers made a valid point.

A player who can come in and produce like that, while costing next to nothing against the salary cap, is a valuable commodity in the NFL. And any one of the 31 other NFL teams can offer Hurleman a spot on its 53-player roster right now, without having to give the Steelers anything in return.

It would be surprising if Hurleman remains on the Steelers’ practice squad for the rest of the season. Either the Steelers will put him on their active roster, or some other team will.

The Packers have made it official — Micah Parsons is back on the roster.

Green Bay announced on Tuesday that Parsons has been activated to the 53-man roster in advance of Wednesday’s game against the Rams.

Parsons has been medically cleared to play. While he’s officially listed as questionable, all signs point to the edge rusher returning from his torn ACL this week in Los Angeles.

In 14 games for the Packers last year, Parsons tallied 12.5 sacks with 12 tackles for loss and 27 quarterback hits. He also had one pass defensed and two forced fumbles.

Though he wasn’t able to finish the 2025 season, he was still an AP first-team All-Pro for the third time in his career.

Green Bay had an open spot on its 53-man roster, so the club did not need to make a corresponding move.

‘Hexed’ Creatives on Telling a Modern-Day Witchcraft Story, Casting Hailee Steinfeld, and Their Favorite Easter Eggs

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

SPOILER ALERT: This story discusses mild plot points for “Hexed,” opening in theaters on Wednesday.

Walt Disney Animation Studios’ 65th film “Hexed” tells the story of the hidden world of witches and how, throughout history, they have been misunderstood. At its heart, however, is a story of motherhood and the powerful bond between a mother and daughter.

Disney animated films often kill off a parent to propel a story forward. But the creatives behind “Hexed” embraced that important relationship. Related Stories ‘PAW Patrol’ Spinoff Series ‘Skye’s Pup Squad’ Set for 2027 Release

Directed by Fawn Veerasunthorn and Jason Hand, and co-directed by Josie Trinidad, the film follows Billie (Hailee Steinfeld), a rebellious teen who escapes into the secret world of Hexe, a realm of witches and wizards who live in harmony with nature.

Veerasunthorn explained that the story idea was originally pitched by Trinidad. “She wanted to tell a story about the hidden world of witches. What if there’s a place where they can thrive on their own? The place built by witches for witches.” More importantly, Veerasunthorn notes, Trinidad wanted to tell a story of motherhood and the true heart of the story lies in the complicated, relatable relationship between Billie and her mother, Alice (Rashida Jones).

In developing Billie, she was portrayed as a modern teenager who wears combat boots and has an edgy style. Producer Yvett Merino explains that Billie oozes confidence: “She doesn’t quite fit in. There’s something that feels different about her.”

The key was making the film’s main protagonist relatable. “We all go through that at some point in our lives, especially in those teenage years where you’re just trying to figure out who you are,” Merino says. “She takes her mom’s car. She borrows her mom’s car to go and free the school mascot. She’s imperfect, impulsive and maybe doesn’t always make the best decisions.” Merino goes on to say, “When I was a teenager, I didn’t always make the best decisions. But you know what? I got away with it. We’ll just learn from that lesson.”

Imperfect relationships were key when it came to Alice as well. Alice is protective of Billie and wants to keep her daughter safe. Hand points out, “We’re all imperfect. We’re all trying our hardest, but we’re still figuring it out and that was really important to us.”

Visually, the world of “Hexed” is vibrant and it took over 700 artists to bring it to life, sometimes with up to 40 people working on a sequence. “The animation style pushed boundaries as far as they could by creating a world audiences had not seen before, filled with characters they’d never seen,” Veerasunthorn says.

The idea was that it would feel like a crisp autumn day after it had just rained and the air felt a bit chilly. “It’s a perfect place where magic can thrive,” she adds.

Producer Roy Conli credits the film’s production designer, Lorelay Bové, for her creativity. “When people see this film, they will see a world they’ve never seen; they’ll see characters they’ve never seen before in a Disney film.”

If the textures of Hexe look heightened, that was a deliberate choice. “We turn the dial up so it feels more like a caricature drawing could feel,” Hand explains. “We wanted this world to be so enticing to Billie. All of our decisions within the world of Hexe go back to Billie. When she goes out into that city for the first time, what’s she going to want to see? Why is this world built for her? What is it speaking to her inside?” Hand says. All those factors were important from both a production design standpoint, but also from a character standpoint. “And then for that journey to bring her mom back into it, throws her for a loop. That’s where the story starts to get its juice and interesting.”

Voice casting was important too. Hand recalls seeing Steinfeld in “True Grit” and later in “The Edge of Seventeen.” In that film, Steinfeld plays a troubled teen who had a contentious relationship with their mom. He says, “I always loved her in that role. I still loved her, even though she was doing the worst things and saying terrible stuff and that’s a really great quality.” But it was her voice quality that made her perfect to lead “Hexed.” “Our animators even picked up on particular mannerisms of the way she holds her mouth, little micro expressions that she does.”

Jones was perfect for Alice. Verasunthorn says, “Rashida brings this sense of comedy in a very uptight mom. Those two words don’t go well together, but somehow she makes it work. The depth of vulnerability that we get to see in Alice is quite beautiful.”

“Hexed” wouldn’t be a Disney movie without a villain to stir up a little trouble. Enter Jodie Foster, who voices Queen Celeste, a sinister ruler who stops at nothing to maintain her power.

Was it easy to get her to say yes? Conli says, “We asked her and she did it. She’d never played a villain before. She’s done a phenomenal job at this incredible narcissistic witch that is a little over the top.”

Joining Steinfeld, Jones and Foster are Walton Goggins as Beef Roger Krumchuk, a three-eyed cat.

“He brought so much comedy and so much personality, way beyond what we ever imagined,” Merino says.

As for the film’s Easter eggs, there are plenty to look out for. Among them are Aladdin’s magic carpet in Billie’s bedroom, a floating head fortuneteller that nods to “The Haunted Mansion.” Mother Gothel from “Tangled” appears in a blink-and-you’ll-miss-it moment.

Hand’s favorite one is Cursula, a nod to “The Little Mermaid’s” Ursula. “It’s voiced by Jodi Benson — who provided the voice of Ariel in the original animated film. I love ‘The Little Mermaid’ so much, and so to have Jodi Benson come in and do a complete twist on it and have her play Cursula, she was so amazing. She gave us such energy.”

Merino and Conli concur that the scene with dancing brooms is one that is special for both of them. “It reaches back to our history and goes back to Walt Disney’s “Fantasia,” and the Sorcerer’s Apprentice scene,” Merino says.

“The brooms, that’s 80 years in the past. That’s ‘Fantasia’; it touches on this amazing legacy that we all live with, and we all celebrate.”

‘Naughty’ vs ‘Nasty’: Universal and Warner Bros. Battle Over Titles for New Jennifer Aniston, Jenna Ortega Movies (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Two similarly titled movies are causing a war of words between Universal and Warner Bros. Pictures. The legacy studios are locked in a stalemate over dueling projects that sound alike and are set to hit theaters around the same time, five sources tell Variety.

The first is “Naughty,” Olivia Wilde’s raucous Christmas comedy starring Jennifer Aniston. The other is “Nasty,” Mary Bronstein’s psychological drama led by Jenna Ortega, which will roll out under Warner’s new indie label Clockwork. Universal slated “Naughty” for Nov. 5, 2027, after registering the name with the Motion Picture Association in 2023, according to two sources. Clockwork, the nascent shingle run by Neon veteran Christian Parkes, has not officially set a date for “Nasty,” though four sources say the film is also eyeing a November 2027 release. Related Stories ‘PAW Patrol’ Spinoff Series ‘Skye’s Pup Squad’ Set for 2027 Release

Generally, studios go to great lengths to avoid delivering movies with similar-sounding names — much less launching them in the same corridor. “Naughty” and “Nasty” aren’t alike in genre or subject matter. “Naughty,” featuring Aniston and Peter Dinklage, follows a single mom who has to convince Santa Claus to testify at her divorce hearing. “Nasty,” Bronstein’s follow-up to “If I Had Legs I’d Kick You,” revolves around an elite gymnast competing for a spot on the Olympic team while dealing with her manipulative coach.

Yet insiders at Universal and Warner Bros. feel the fact that the titles are basically synonyms could confuse moviegoers. Further complicating the matter is that LuckyChap, Margot Robbie’s prolific film and TV production shop, is backing both projects. Universal and Clockwork declined to comment.

The spat is headed toward a formal review by the MPA, which has a 101-year-old department called the Title Registration Bureau to manage such conflicts. Arbitration involves a panel of studio intellectual property lawyers at the ready to hash it out. Although titles are not copyrightable, the TRB is armed with a 50-page document outlining rules of play and precedent, per one source, and its rulings are legally binding. The MPA declined to comment on the matter.

“Movies want to distinguish themselves, and the first way they do that is the title,” says Jeff Bock, an analyst with Exhibitor Relations. “If they sound similar, there could be confusion.”

Universal believes it should have dibs since it was first to file with “Naughty.” That would mean “Nasty” either changes its name or shifts its release date. Insiders note Clockwork filed two alternative titles for “Nasty” with its MPA documentation, but the filmmakers are against using the backups.

Meanwhile, sources close to “Nasty” feel that Universal is “punching down” at Clockwork because the indie drama doesn’t have the same broad commercial appeal as the Aniston-led holiday comedy. (Though Clockwork is a specialty label, it is owned and operated by the vast Skydance Motion Picture Group). Wilde is content to let “Nasty” proceed as titled, according to a person familiar with her thinking. LuckyChap is working toward a solution that sets up both projects for success without entering MPA arbitration, two sources add.

Disputes like these rarely become public knowledge, insiders say. And most often, studios work it out without involving the Title Registration Bureau. The group’s last two arbitrations were in 2018. That’s when Disney and Lucasfilm settled on “Solo: A Star Wars Story” rather than “Solo” to distinguish the Han Solo origin story from the 2018 climbing documentary “Free Solo.” And Universal agreed to change its raunchy comedy to “Blockers” from “The Pact,” a name that was already used by a 2012 horror film.

Renaming movies can benefit both parties. Ask Paramount and Sony, which released “No Strings Attached” and “Friends With Benefits” a few months apart in 2011. Both romantic comedies — one starring Natalie Portman and Ashton Kutcher and the other with Mila Kunis and Justin Timberlake — had an identical premise involving two friends who agree to casual sex without dating. “No Strings Attached” was originally called “Friends With Benefits,” but the studio acquiesced to the title change. Any plot similarities didn’t dilute the films’ respective box office, with each one grossing $149 million globally.

To that end, movies with similar themes or ideas are less of a problem for studios. Bock points to 1998’s “Armageddon” and “Deep Impact,” two disaster films about comets on a collision course with Earth. Both were theatrical winners, with “Armageddon” earning $553 million globally and “Deep Impact” generating $350 million globally.

“Consumers might be fooled by something like that, but it didn’t affect the box office,” he says. “They turned out to be different, even though the synopsis was the same.”

Montel Williams Gets Candid About the State of Daytime TV: “The Talk Shows of My Era Became the Reality Shows of Today”

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The former 'Montel Williams Show' host talks about how short attention spans have changed the daytime talk show landscape during a conversation on the 'We're Out of Time' podcast with host Richard Taite.

Montel Williams is getting candid about the state of daytime talk show, saying viewers’ attention spans have changed a lot since he ended his own talker in 2008.

“It’s a sad commentary that we lost our attention span in the last 15 years,” he said in a conversation with We’re Out of Time podcast host Richard Taite, debuting Tuesday. “The talk shows that I did and the talk shows of that era were hourlong talk discussions. We don’t have the patience anymore to listen to a topic for an hour. I say the majority of people don’t.” Related Stories TV 'Property Brother' Jonathan Scott Is Getting a Daytime Talk Show TV Montel Williams Hospitalized After Workout, Spokesman Says

He said the format has morphed quite a bit since his show ended. (The Montel Williams Show ran from July 1991 to May 2008, spanning 17 seasons.)

“So the talk shows of my era became the reality shows of today,” he opined. “I’ll say that again, the talk shows of my era became the reality shows of today. We took the dysfunction that we put on display and turned those into individual shows of their own, to the point now that the talk that’s out there right now, what do they really even talk? Other than selling products and hawking a new movie or hawking a new record, what do they do? Is there any discussion that makes man better?”

Williams was quick to say he’s not criticizing anyone in particular, just nothing how times have changed.

“I’m not casting aspersion on my peers of the day,” he said. “But I am saying that at least back when we talked about issues, we talked about issues that changed the world, that I believe changed America. We were talking about AIDS before any news stations were going full reports on it. We were talking about civil unrest, we were talking about racism, we were talking about hate, we were talking about all those things that we didn’t even talk about it around the dinner table. We talked about it. Some of us did it in the salacious way. But at least they had a discussion.”

Williams added that the goal with his show was to educate people and put a spotlight on a topic that viewers perhaps hadn’t been introduced to or thought that much about. In other words, something that would “make you stop and think,” he said.

Does he think that kind of talk show would work today?

“It could, if it was formatted differently,” he says. “I think we can go an entire hour, but we have to go in that hour introducing every single piece of that topic, not just one piece, but we have to introduce it all and add to it information that people can take away.”

Taite likened that to scrolling on your phone, where you’re staying engaged by absorbing different information — and a lot of it — in a short time span.

“That’s right, and [keeping] you stimulated on the same topic instead of scrolling, or swiping up and down, instead of doing that and getting a different topic every single second, and that’s what they’re trying to do,” Williams replied. “They’re trying to give you something different every five or six minutes where they never really even solved the first five minutes. I say that every single topic out there can be broken into 20 different topics. Somebody needs to just program it that way.”

He added, “I think we may gravitate back to that as soon as we realize that we pushed attention span to a point where it’s no longer attention span.”

The full episode of We’re Out of Time with Montel Williams and host Richard Taite premieres at noon PT on Tuesday on YouTube, Spotify and Apple Podcasts.

Derek Hough Recalls Difficult Utah Upbringing with Neighbors Who ‘Terrorized’ Family Before Moving to London

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Derek Hough is opening up about his upbringing, sharing painful details from his childhood in his new Hulu documentary, “The Symphony of Dance.”

In the film, the “Dancing with the Stars" judge returns to his childhood home in Utah, and details the challenging environment he was raised in.

“The neighborhood that we grew up in, we definitely just had some neighbors that weren’t great,” Derek, 41, explained. “They pretty much terrorized my family in a lot of ways.”

“They hung me up in a tree by my ankles. They put a gun to my head, spat on me, hogtied me up in a field and left me there,” he continued. “One of my neighbors was, for lack of a better word, really inappropriate with me, physically.”

Eventually, Derek’s parents separated, and the family moved across town to an apartment building where his mother and father lived in units across the hall from one another.

“I remember moving away from there and feeling relieved,” he said. However, the move didn’t solve all of his problems, and he continued to struggle in the wake of his parents’ divorce.

“I just didn’t understand where I was going. I didn’t understand what I was doing,” he said. “I was just kind of left to my own devices as a 12-year-old. I would skip school. I would skip dance, just floating around with no direction.”

Although he had initially pushed back when his parents wanted to enroll him in dance classes, Derek explained that the artform eventually became his “safe haven”—and his ticket to a new life in London.

When renowned dancers Corky and Shirley Ballas—who “Dancing with the Stars” fans will recognize as the parents of pro Mark Ballas—visited Derek’s dance studio, they saw that he had something special.

“We saw the gift, we saw the talent, and we saw the struggle that his family was having at the time,” Shirley recalled in her documentary interview. “But more than that, he showed his dedication to the art. You could see it from a very, very young age.”

Shirley explained that this potential is what motivated the Ballas family to take Derek under their wings.

“He needed an opportunity. He needed a place to grow. And I remember him walking in, after the end of one of the classes, and saying to me, ‘Can I come and live with you?’” Shirley said. “We spoke to [his father] Bruce, he thought that was a good idea for Derek to live with us in London for a short space of time.”

The arrangement—in which Derek was enrolled in the Italia Conti Performing Arts Academy with Mark—was initially planned to last just six months. But Derek’s father said that by Christmas of that year, the Ballas family suggested bringing sister Julianne Hough over, too.

At the time, Julianne was 10 and Derek was 12.

“I felt like moving away was great because I was getting away from something,” Derek explained. “I was leaving like, this wounded boy, and I was going to go become this success. I was gonna work hard and achieve things and overcome things.”

The stay kept getting extended as Derek became more dedicated to ballroom dancing.

“I every year, sort of begged them to come home, and they begged to stay,” Bruce said of his children’s move to London.

Derek and Julianne felt very at home overseas, however.

“It was almost like this ritual. Every summer we would go home, and my dad was like, ‘Yo, it’s time to come home, You guys. I wanna raise you. You’re my kids. I want you to be here,” Derek said. “And it was honestly this fight where I was like, ‘We’re on this path, and I’m gonna win this championship if I keep going here and going in this direction.”

Derek would ultimately stay in London—and win a handful of titles—until 2007, when he returned to the United States full time and joined the cast of “Dancing with the Stars.”

I Learned How Baby Carrots Are Made &amp; Haven't Bought Them Since—Here's Why

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

I have some very strong feelings about a very small vegetable.

As a lover of all veggies, I love every type of carrot out there—except packaged baby carrots. That’s right: those bright orange cylinders with suspiciously perfect rounded corners that lurk in vending machines, school lunchboxes, and convenience store cold cases across our nation. They might be easy to eat on the go and better for you than a bag of Cheetos, but there’s a lot more going on behind the scenes of this seemingly simple healthy snack.

Here’s the truth about baby carrots—and why you might want to think twice about snagging a bag.

The bag might claim otherwise, but packaged “baby carrots” are typically larger carrots chopped into 2-inch pieces, then peeled and polished down to that perfect snackable size. Real baby carrots are immature carrots harvested before they reach full size. They’re sold in bunches—unpeeled, often with the tops still attached.

While a whole carrot has a delicate, nuanced sweetness, the taste of these so-called baby carrots is best described as... factory-fresh. Don’t believe me? Grab a bag of regular carrots and do a side-by-side taste test.

Baby carrots were originally “invented” in the 1980s to make misshapen or broken carrots sellable. This was a smart way to reduce food waste and save money, but it also helped reinforce the unrealistic expectation that produce must look perfect in order to taste good. Today, baby-cut carrots are grown and processed to meet specific standards for size and shape, all to produce those identical little orange cylinders.

Here’s the bottom line: When you buy a bag of baby carrots, you’re buying into a world that’s seemingly as obsessed with unnatural perfection as the modern-day beauty industry.

Energy conservation is top of mind for consumers these days, but making baby carrots look so uniform takes a lot of machinery. After they’re sorted, they chop the carrots into roughly 2-inch pieces and shave them—twice. Then, they go through a rough cutter, which gives them a general shape, before being polished to create those unrealistically perfect rounded edges. That’s a lot of extra work to make something that was completely edible to begin with look a certain way.

To prevent bacterial growth, baby carrots may be rinsed with a diluted chlorine solution before packaging. They’re then rinsed with water, and the chlorine treatment itself is an “acceptable” food-safety practice which doesn’t make baby carrots unsafe to eat. Still, do you really want your “fresh” snack to have undergone chlorine treatments of any kind? For me, that’s a hard no, and I’d rather buy whole carrots and wash and cut them myself.

Because baby carrots have been peeled and cut, they're more prone to drying out and developing something called “carrot blush,” a thin white film caused by dehydration. The film itself is harmless, but it certainly doesn’t make them look appetizing. Worse, they can turn slimy inside the bag, even before you open it. And that’s downright gross.

Prices vary depending on where you shop, but baby-cut carrots can cost more per pound than regular whole carrots. You're essentially paying extra for someone else to handle the peeling, cutting, and polishing. For a task that takes barely any time at home, I'd rather keep the difference in my wallet.

Seriously. Enjoy the taste and texture of whole carrots, potentially save some money, and skip the extra processing by simply buying a bag of whole carrots and cutting them yourself. It’ll take you less time than you spent reading this article.

2 Montgomery County magnet stories raise 1 question about access

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

“Minorities in Magnets” was the winner of the ABA Section of Civil Rights and Social Justice’s 2026 High School Civil Rights Film Competition.

A high school filmmaker saw a problem in Montgomery County, Maryland’s selective programs: Lack of representation of students of color had an impact on their educational experiences. At nearly the same time, a lawsuit challenging admissions to the county’s middle school magnet programs reached the U.S. Supreme Court.

Although these stories involve different programs and raise different questions, they share an important issue: How do we determine whether an admissions system provides fair access to educational opportunities, and what evidence should matter?

The ABA Section of Civil Rights and Social Justice recognized Minorities in Magnets, directed by Toftaa Fedhaa, as the first place winner of the section’s 2026 High School Civil Rights Film Competition. Her film explores the experiences of students of color in the Montgomery County Public Schools’ high school magnet programs and the effects of their underrepresentation.

Separately, on Oct. 19, the Supreme Court agreed to hear Association for Education Fairness v. Montgomery County Board of Education. The case concerns admissions to four selective middle school magnet programs, not the high school programs discussed in the film.

While the court did not rule on whether the district’s admissions policy is discriminatory, it agreed to consider a narrower legal question about the evidence plaintiffs must present when challenging a facially neutral admissions policy as racially discriminatory.

The Association for Education Fairness alleges that Montgomery County’s admissions policies intentionally disadvantaged Asian American students. The school district maintains that its admissions system is designed to provide equitable access to rigorous educational opportunities.

After initially challenging a “field test,” the plaintiffs challenged a revised lottery-based system introduced for the 2021-2022 school year. That system screened students for their potential to benefit from enriched instruction and then used a lottery to assign students to magnet programs or enriched instruction at their neighborhood schools.

The federal district court dismissed the challenge to the revised plan, finding that the plaintiffs had not adequately alleged discriminatory effect or intent. The 4th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals at Richmond, Virginia, affirmed, applying circuit precedent on disparate impact allegations.

The Supreme Court will now consider whether a plaintiff challenging a facially neutral admissions policy must demonstrate that the affected group experienced an aggregate “disparate impact” compared with its share of the applicant pool.

Put simply, “disparate impact” refers to a policy that produces unequal outcomes for a particular group, even when the policy does not expressly classify individuals by race. An equal protection challenge also raises the question of discriminatory purpose.

The court’s decision will address the legal standards governing these types of claims and whether a particular showing of aggregate impact is required before such a claim may proceed, not whether Montgomery County’s policy ultimately violates the Constitution. The court has not yet decided the merits of the case.

The film underscores why the conversation should not only focus exclusively on admissions policies, statistics and legal standards but should also consider the voices and experiences of students.

The file and the lawsuit should not be confused or treated as evidence of one another. They involve different grade levels, programs and legal questions. The experiences described in the film do not establish what happened in the middle school admissions process, just as the allegations in the lawsuit do not resolve the concerns raised by high school students.

Although selective educational programs can open doors to advanced coursework, specialized instruction and opportunities that shape a student’s academic future, simply making a program available to everyone does not necessarily mean everyone has an equal opportunity to participate.

We must also consider who knows these programs exist, how students are identified and encouraged to apply, what barriers may discourage participation, and whether students feel represented and welcome once they are admitted.

These are important questions for educators, families, policymakers and communities to consider. They may also inform legal analysis, although the applicable legal standards determine what evidence is relevant to a particular claim.

For lawyers, the Supreme Court case highlights the importance of understanding how statistical evidence, applicant pools and allegations of discriminatory intent intersect when challenging facially neutral policies. It also reinforces the need to examine the specific admissions process and how that process may have changed over time. The court’s decision may affect how courts evaluate statistical evidence in challenges to facially neutral admissions policies.

For school officials, families and advocates, the broader questions are equally important:

• How are students identified and informed about selective educational opportunities?

• What criteria determine eligibility and admission?

• Are admissions outcomes regularly examined across schools and student populations?

• Are the policies and measures used to assess access transparent and understandable?

These questions deserve attention regardless of how the Supreme Court ultimately resolves the legal issue before it.

The case is also a reminder that we must distinguish between allegations in a lawsuit, a school district’s stated reasons for its policies, procedural court decisions, and a final ruling on the merits. Understanding those distinctions is essential to an informed discussion.

The winning film, Minorities in Magnets, brings student perspectives into a debate that is often reduced to admissions criteria and demographic statistics. The Supreme Court will address a specific legal question about the evidence required in an equal protection challenge.

Neither alone answers the broader question of what meaningful educational opportunity should look like. But they invite a broader conversation about how communities define opportunity, how they measure access, and how they respond when students say the system is not working for them.

As lawyers and members of our communities, we have an obligation to listen, ask difficult questions, and examine whether the systems we create are providing genuine opportunities for all students.

Ultimately, educational access is about more than who gets admitted. It is also about who gets the opportunity to succeed, who feels that they belong, and whether every student has a fair chance to reach their full potential.

Mario A. Sulllivan is the immediate past chair of the ABA Section of Civil Rights and Social Justice and a name partner at Johnson & Sullivan in Chicago.

This column reflects the opinions of the author and not necessarily the views of the ABA Journal—or the American Bar Association.
