Greg Hovanessian & Lindsey Normington To Lead Psychological Thriller ‘Nightwaves’

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Greg Hovanessian (The Mist, Cardinal) and Lindsey Normington (Margo’s Got Money Troubles, Anora) are set to lead the new psychological thriller Nightwaves, from writer and director Kristian McKay (Do Not Open, Kill Trip) and Obscura Film. Cherie Currie (Foxes, Twilight Zone: The Movie) and Shane Coffey (Pretty Little Liars, Good Girls) will also star.

Set against the dark, desolate highways of Texas, Nightwaves unfolds over the course of a single night, following Mason (Hovanessian), a young man driving alone whose unexpected phone conversation with a mysterious woman (Normington) offers an initially welcome escape. As their exchange shifts from flirtation to increasingly unsettling questions, the woman on the other end of the line begins to threaten everything Mason is desperately trying to leave behind. With each passing mile, he finds himself moving closer to a confrontation he cannot simply hang up to avoid. Related Stories Casting Jillian Moray Sets Directorial Debut 'Pismo,' Stars Opposite Nuha Jes Izman, Madeline Popovich & More News 'Hunger Games: Sunrise On The Reaping' Heading To Potential Franchise-Record Monday With $12M

Currie and Coffey’s roles are under wraps. Watch on Deadline

Production is currently underway in San Antonio and the surrounding Texas landscape.

“There’s something incredibly intimate about talking to someone in the dark when you can’t see their face. You start filling in the blanks, and that can be seductive or terrifying,” shared McKay. “With Nightwaves, I want the audience to feel that connection tightening until it becomes a trap. Greg and Lindsey bring the vulnerability and unpredictability these roles demand, and having Cherie and Shane in this world gives the film such a distinct personality.”

Obscura Film touts in a release how “McKay will bring a distinctive visual approach to the thriller, allowing tension and performances to unfold in extended, uninterrupted moments. Using the empty stretches of highway, passing headlights, and the confined interior of a moving car, the film transforms an ordinary phone conversation into an increasingly claustrophobic psychological experience, where what remains unseen may be more terrifying than anything in view.”

The film is produced by Obscura Film partners Daunesh Alcott and McKay, alongside Jose Corres. Executive produced by Gustavo Oliveira.

Nightwaves expands Obscura Film’s growing slate of independently produced genre features. The project follows Kill Trip, acquired by Quiver Distribution and released in 2026, and Do Not Open, McKay’s supernatural thriller starring Freddy Carter, Lulu Wilson, Mark McKenna, John Ford Coley and Stelio Savante, which is currently in post-production.

Hovanessian is repped by Robert Stein Management and Clear Talent Group. Normington is repped by Innovative Artists and Authentic Talent & Literary Management. Coffey is repped by Industry Entertainment and Pakula/ King.

Marvel’s ‘Avengers: Doomsday’ Won’t Screen for BAFTA, Golden Globes and Other Early Awards Groups to Guard Its Spoilers (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Earth’s Mightiest Heroes will fight Doctor Doom in the latest Avengers installment, but they won’t face the first wave of critics and major guilds on the awards calendar.

Marvel Studios’ “Avengers: Doomsday” will not screen for awards voting bodies before its world premiere, Variety has learned exclusively, a move designed to protect its theatrical run by keeping the film’s closely guarded plot turns out of the spoiler ecosystem ahead of its Dec. 18 theatrical release. The film premieres Dec. 14 in Los Angeles, just four days before it opens nationwide. Related Stories Dear Oscars: Don’t Let Laika and ‘Wildwood’ Fail. There Is No Backup Stop-Motion Studio Waiting in the Wings Oscar Predictions: Production Design — Could Something as Innovative and Cool as 'Backrooms' Find Love?

The strategy follows a well-worn playbook for event films of this scale, from “Star Wars: The Force Awakens” to “Spider-Man: No Way Home.” But protecting the surprises comes at a cost, with “Doomsday” set to sit out a long stretch of the precursor season as voting windows close before most awards voters have seen a frame. The biggest question is: What does that mean for the Oscars?

The most consequential window belongs to the Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences. Preliminary voting for the Oscar shortlists begins Monday, Dec. 7, and closes Dec. 11, with shortlists in 12 categories announced on Dec. 15, the day after the film’s premiere. Those categories are animated short film, casting, cinematography, documentary feature, documentary short, international feature, live action short, makeup and hairstyling, original score, original song, sound and visual effects. “Doomsday” could factor into as many as seven of them.

Visual effects is where “Doomsday” has the clearest path because of how that race is structured, and the studio’s previous successes in the category (i.e., “Guardians of the Galaxy,” “Black Panther: Wakanda Forever”). Before the official 10-film shortlist is announced Dec. 15, the field is narrowed to 20 semifinalists, a list the Academy does not make public, though Variety has reported it exclusively in past seasons. That list is expected to take shape in the coming days.

Under current Academy rules, the president appoints a chair to preside over an executive committee of active and life members of the Visual Effects Branch. That committee reviews a list of eligible films and, through a series of meetings, selects up to 20 for further consideration. Achievements are judged on “the contribution the visual effects make to the overall production” and “the human artistry and skill, fidelity and technical achievement with which the visual illusions are achieved.”

Crucially, the rules build in flexibility that limits the risk of a major spoiler leak. The executive committee “will request that producers provide opportunities to view their films through advance screenings,” and if a film isn’t available two weeks before the selection meeting, excerpts may be submitted instead. If suitable materials aren’t made available, the committee can deem the film ineligible.

It’s not clear whether Marvel Studios screened the full film for the committee or submitted excerpts. Nonetheless, Variety has confirmed that “Doomsday” was submitted to the relevant Academy branches for consideration, along with the materials required for shortlist eligibility.

In other words, a far smaller group viewing a controlled screening or a curated reel gives Marvel a way to stay in the VFX conversation without exposing its third act to hundreds of voters (The Visual Effects Branch is among the top five largest of the Academy). That matters for a studio that has landed visual effects nominations for “Avengers: Infinity War,” “Avengers: Endgame” and “Spider-Man: No Way Home” but is still searching for its first win in the category.

In Variety’s most recent Oscar predictions, “Avengers: Doomsday” is among the early projected five film visual effects nominees alongside “Dune: Part Three,” “Project Hail Mary” and “Wildwood.”

What remains unclear is whether voters in the other branches where “Doomsday” could compete, including cinematography, makeup and hairstyling, original score and sound, will see the full film before the Dec. 11 deadline.

The rest of the early calendar has tougher hurdles to overcome.

The first casualty already came on Nov. 23, when the Critics Choice Association’s Below the Line Committee announced its shortlists. “Doomsday” was not screened for the committee in time for deliberations, and neither was Sony Pictures’ “Jumanji: Open World.” With no member screenings before Critics Choice nominations are announced on Dec. 4, the film will not factor into that ballot either.

Across the pond, BAFTA reveals its full slate of eligible films on Tuesday, moved up from its original Thursday date. Films must be made available on the BAFTA viewing portal to nearly 14,000 members around the globe and “Doomsday” will not be on the platform, in part to protect its theatrical window, according to sources. That effectively removes the film from this year’s BAFTA longlist consideration. Round one of BAFTA voting runs from Dec. 4 to Jan. 4.

The Golden Globes are also off the table. Voting in the film categories opens Tuesday, and closes Dec. 4, the same day Critics Choice nominations are revealed. Without a screening, “Doomsday” will be absent from every category, most notably Cinematic and Box Office Achievement, which recognizes films that gross more than $150 million worldwide, a threshold the film is projected to clear with ease.

“Doomsday” will also bypass the New York Film Critics Circle, the National Board of Review, the Los Angeles Film Critics Association, the AFI Awards’ top 10 and the Casting Society of America’s Artios Awards.

Not every road outside the Academy is blocked.

Nomination voting for the Actor Awards, formerly the SAG Awards, runs from Dec. 14 to Jan. 3, and members of the nominating committee will have opportunities to see the film in theaters. That keeps the door mostly ajar for the stunt ensemble and for Robert Downey Jr., the Oscar winner for “Oppenheimer,” whose turn as Doctor Doom is the film’s biggest swing.

There is also precedent for a December blockbuster surviving this exact calendar. “Star Wars: The Force Awakens” held its world premiere on Dec. 14, 2015, opened Dec. 18 and went on to earn five Oscar nominations. “Spider-Man: No Way Home” premiered Dec. 13, 2021, opened four days later and landed a visual effects nomination.

Marvel recently dropped the final trailer, which arrives after “Avengers: Endgame Encore” lit up the box office with a No. 1 domestic debut of $26 million. The re-release of the 2019 blockbuster included four new scenes that tie into “Doomsday” and added $143 million globally, making “Endgame” the highest-grossing movie of all time. In the wake of the record-breaking “Spider-Man: Brand New Day,” Disney and Marvel are expecting “Doomsday” to be another massive superhero hit.

“Doomsday” brings back Downey as the villainous Doctor Doom, who appears to be a variant of Tony Stark/Iron Man from another universe. The sprawling ensemble reaches across the MCU and beyond, with several characters from Fox’s “X-Men” films officially crossing over into the franchise’s main timeline.

Alongside Downey, Chris Evans returns as Steve Rogers/Captain America, joining Chris Hemsworth as Thor, Paul Rudd as Ant-Man, Letitia Wright as Black Panther, Sebastian Stan as the Winter Soldier, Tom Hiddleston as Loki and Simu Liu as Shang-Chi. The cast of “The Fantastic Four: First Steps” also returns, with Pedro Pascal as Mister Fantastic, Vanessa Kirby as the Invisible Woman, Joseph Quinn as the Human Torch and Ebon Moss-Bachrach as the Thing, reuniting to face their longtime nemesis. Florence Pugh reprises her role as Yelena Belova following her appearance in “Spider-Man: Brand New Day,” alongside her “Thunderbolts” co-stars David Harbour as Red Guardian, Lewis Pullman as Sentry, Wyatt Russell as U.S. Agent and Hannah John-Kamen as Ghost.

The film also marks a major crossover with Fox’s “X-Men” universe, bringing back Patrick Stewart as Professor Charles Xavier, Ian McKellen as Magneto, James Marsden as Cyclops, Kelsey Grammer as Beast, Rebecca Romijn as Mystique, Alan Cumming as Nightcrawler and Channing Tatum as Gambit.

10 Mad Magazine Mascot Alfred P. Neuman Quotes To Live By

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The humor periodical Mad started off as a comic in 1952, before taking on its more recognizable magazine with 1955's Mad #24. That cover featured a prominent appearance by the caricature of a character who would come to be known as Alfred E. Neuman. A few issues later, on the cover of Mad #30, Alfred took center stage, officially becoming the magazine's figurehead and mascot.

From its inception, issues of Mad included an epigraph, a quote from a historical figure, politician, or celebrity that set the tone. The first time Mad opened with a quote attributed to Alfred E. Neuman was Mad #38. From that point on, Alfred has had the first, and definitive, line in practically every issue of the magazine since.

Sometimes Alfred's quotes are bawdy, and sometimes they are politically charged. Often, though, they are low-key life lessons, the kind of wisdom that only a comic character can get away with sharing.

"It's a good idea to keep your words soft and sweet because you never know when you'll have to eat them!" Here, Alfred E. Neuman riffs on a centuries-old idiom. Be careful what comes out of your mouth, Neuman warns, because if what you say ends up getting shoved back in your face, down your own throat, you might find yourself choking on the tone and the meanings of your words.

It is sensible advice, especially for comedians, but knowing Alfred, and knowing Mad magazine, this is really a "do as I say, not as I do" case of cautionary advice.

"You'll never get rid of a bad temper by losing it!" There's another saying, which is that you can't control your immediate reaction to something, but you can control your reaction to the reaction. If you're prone to getting angry, giving into it is only going to make the problem worse, that's what Alfred E. Neuman is saying here.

It is a slyly clever bit of wordplay. In fact, this bon mot could be remixed into a riddle. "What can you never get rid of by losing it?" "A bad temper!" The cover for this issue, Mad #71, shows Alfred's mug at the center of a dartboard, and with its opening quote at least, the magazine hit the comedic bullseye.

"Learn from the mistakes of others." That is age-old wisdom. But why? That part is usually left unsaid, but leave it to Alfred E. Neuman to say the quiet part loud. It is "'cause you'll never live long enough to make 'em all yourself!"

Before it was a film franchise, National Lampoon was a groundbreaking American humor magazine; these are the greatest covers from its 3-decade history

Life is finite. There's only so much failure and foible the average person can cram into one lifetime. Recognizing and avoiding others' failures isn't about being more likely to succeed in your own endeavors. It is simply a time-saving exercise. Plus, in some cases, those mistakes might have been fatal, and avoiding them is the only way to guarantee a long life full non-lethal errors in judgment.

The art of the one-liner is trickier than people give it credit for. (Just ask Henny Youngman.) In just a few words, the speaker has to set up and execute the punchline, but there is more to it than that. The line has to conjure an image in people's heads. And that image can be vague, or it can be concrete.

Here, it is literally the latter. "Some minds are like concrete," Alfred E. Neuman says in Mad #82, in the sense that they are "all mixed up and permanently set." In other words, some people are defiantly confused. Obstinately misguided. Flat out wrong and unwilling to revise their opinions. Don't be like those people, Alfred suggests. Be willing to admit your wrong.

Long before Gordan Gecko told the world "greed is good" in Wall Street, Alfred E. Neuman gave Mad readers the real truth about cold hard cash. "Sometimes, the best scheme for doubling your money is to fold it in half and stuff it back in your wallet," Neuman declared in Mad #87.

Meaning, get-rich-quick schemes and seemingly lucrative investments are often not all they seemed cracked up to be. Everyone wants to hustle, and maximize their earnings, but sometimes the smart thing to do is just stack paper. Save up. For Alfred, a folded dollar is worth more than one that is tied up in escrow.

The funny thing about Alfred E. Neuman's advice is that he was often both the angel and the devil on readers' shoulders. His line in Mad #145 exemplifies this. "Sometimes it pays to resist a temptation," he begins. Sounds totally reasonable, and also morally sound, right?

Well, let him finish. Because Alfred also answers why it pays. Not because having will power and self-control are important. Not because resisting temptation is the right thing. No, because temptation is a gamble. Why take the one in front of you when "a better one may be coming along." This hilariously, and cheekily, takes the matter from an ethical equation to a practical matter of when it is the right time to do the wrong thing.

"Experience is something you never have until just after you need it!" This quote from Mad #204 is the perfect companion to the issue's cover, which parodies the Jaws 2 poster, with Alfred E. Neuman piloting a mechanical shark to sneak up on a jet-skiing girl in a bikini.

In the movie, only Chief Brody has the ability to save the day, because only he has the experience of having fought a killer great white before. For most people, most of the time, the toughest moments of their lives are trial and error, giving them the experience that would have made it easier on them only after the fact.

This quote from Mad #221 evokes the earlier entry on this list from Mad #54. It is another way for Alfred E. Neuman to remind readers: "Watch what you say." Especially if you've already gotten yourself in trouble by talking too much.

"When you're in deep water, it's a good idea to keep your mouth shut!" It is a great case of applying a literal lesson to figurative language. If you're in proverbial deep water, or hot water, or any kind of water, talking, or shouting, is only going to result in one thing. Drowning.

Mad #267's cover features a Top Gun parody, and Alfred E. Neuman's quote in the issue makes it clear what kind of people he'd like to strap to the nose of a fighter jet and send flying. "People on ego trips," who, Alfred says bluntly, "should buy one-way tickets."

It is a slick play on words, but the point doesn't mince any. If you are ego-driven and willing to do whatever it takes to feed your own sense of self, take a hike. Take a vacation. Take off and don't come back. For Mad fans, it is a reminder to check themselves before they wreck themselves, and ruin someone else's day too.

The theme of Mad #293's Alfred E. Neuman quote is the same as the prior entry. And it contains essentially the same valuable life lesson. Which is, life is hard for everyone, so don't go around making it harder.

Or as Alfred puts it: "It's not just the ups and downs that make life difficult. It's the jerks." People who go around being miserable all the time need to do more than just sit down and read a Mad magazine, or a Far Side comic, or even a Garfield, and try to lighten up. They need to think about how they are impacting other people. That's what Mad's irreverent, but often on-point mascot is saying here.

'Blade Runner 2099' Creator Reveals the Biggest Challenge for Prime Video’s New Sci-Fi Series

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

In just a few days, we will be going back to the dystopian future of replicants with a new limited series from Prime Video. Blade Runner 2099 jumps the story forward 50 years and places us in a world where the replicants have finally come out on top and humans have been turned into second-class citizens. The series follows Cora (Hunter Schafer), a human who has gone undercover as a replicant, who works as a blade runner alongside Olwen (Michelle Yeoh), a dying replicant in the last days of her life.

Collider got to speak with creator and showrunner Silka Luisa and director Jonathan van Tulleken about this brand new entry into the sci-fi franchise and how the series differentiates itself from the previous two films in the universe. Van Tulleken reveals the films he drew from as reference for the visual palette of the show, and Luisa reveals the process of what it takes to transfer a story into a new format. Luisa also talks about the big change between replicants and humans and how Cora and Olwen's story gives us insight into the new dynamic between the two groups. Finally, she gives some insight into the show's connection to both Blade Runner and Blade Runner 2049 and why the show decided to lean away from drawing stronger connections to the previous film.

Going into this, what's the most daunting task when it comes to formulating the world of Blade Runner 2099?

JONATHAN VAN TULLEKEN: I think Silka had written on the page this concept of the sunshine noir, and it was beautiful because Ridley [Scott] described his movie as a 40-year-old movie set 40 years in the future. And [Jordan] Cronenweth had looked to Citizen Kane, his cinematographer, and he described Harrison Ford as a Bogart detective story. And I think, for me, when reading what Silka had written, I was going, "Well, what is our touchstone then? What is our 40 years, 40 years ago?" So I was really thinking of those movies from the 70s and 60s, you know, The French Connection by [William] Friedkin and [Alan J.] Pakula's paranoia trilogy.

What makes those films so connected to what Ridley does as well is they've so much tactility; they're based in vérité. It was where cameras were small enough that you could take them out onto the streets. I think that really led into how we wanted to approach the work, which was something that felt very human, in a way that Ridley's feels so human; it's about what it is to be human, and so we wanted to make it feel human. So we built places that were real; we used original old lights; we used the original glass from the films, the lenses. I think that allowed us to build on what Ridley started and what Denis [Villeneuve] started.

Now the replicants are the ones doing the hunting.

SILKA LUISA: I think just from the narrative perspective, obviously, there's a shift from something that is so cinematic and theatrical. It's a really different experience going into a theater and being locked in a room and letting this world wash over you. We knew we were going to be watching this on TV, and so we really had to consider what is the pace of our Blade Runner? There is a certain tempo to both films that is very meditative; I love it, it puts you in a mindset that is that world, so we were always trying to calibrate both in the writing and in the shooting and ultimately in post when we were editing, how do we pace the show?

Can you have it be this much faster heartbeat? Do you also then need to create moments of breath so that you can actually do the score, which is so iconic? Early on in editing, we had cut the episodes really, really tight just to get that propulsion of Cora's story. But we realized we really needed those breaths because without them, it's not really Blade Runner, and so we were always balancing that.

Talking about the show and the specifics of the narrative, I really like that you flip the script on what we know before, making replicants into Blade Runners who hunt humans. But how did switching the narrative of the story change the approach to how you might structure the story with the focus being on the replicants more than on humans, or at least more than in previous stories?

LUISA: Well, I think the biggest shift was that it was an undercover story. I knew because we were doing a TV show, we would have more time, and that meant that there could be a relationship at the center of the show, not just a single character and their journey. And if you want to tell a relationship story, you know, there's been so many great undercover [stories], that Donnie Brasco dynamic felt like that was going to give you a human and a replicant side-by-side, both of them hiding secrets, that would be really rich terrain.

It's interesting that in this show, you're right, you do have more room to develop things, and you have dual protagonists in the form of Cora and Olwen. Can you talk a little bit about how these two characters have a meeting of the minds as the story progresses? Because Olwen is getting older, she's dying, whereas Cora is forced to go undercover and has to be forced to think more like a replicant.

LUISA: Yeah, at the beginning of the season, both of them are really lacking empathy. Olwen because she's a replicant, and that is just something; without childhoods, they don't develop it in the same way. It's one of the defining characteristics between humans and replicants that we know from the films. And Cora is just a human who is just been stripped of her soul, because sometimes you're in these circumstances where you just are, "Go, go, go," and you can't really take a breath to even think about other people because you just are trying to live the next day. [We] wanted to tell a story of two people who really have lost this fundamental part of what the human experience is, and how can they rediscover it together? Or can they? Can a human and a replicant being side-by-side... can that awaken empathy in both of them?

I'm curious because I know this story does have connections to the original film and 2049. So considering the reveal that we get from 2049 about replicants being able to have children, I was always thinking that in the back of my mind. Why didn't you guys lean into that? Are we going to get more of that, or are there hints trickled throughout the show? Because I have thoughts.

I've seen them all, but I don't want to get too deep into spoilers, but I'm curious about your thoughts on that.

LUISA: Well, I definitely wanted to leave 2049 with enough space to tell their story, because what I love about the end of that film is it felt like it left me wanting more. I could imagine different directions for how all those characters' lives continue, and what it meant for the city, what it meant for them individually, which I think is something that Blade Runner always does. In terms of is the focus on replicants being able to have babies? No, because I feel like that is connected to the 2049 story in such a fundamental way that it felt like touching it would be making decisions for how they were to finish that film.

Blade Runner 2099 premieres November 25 on Prime Video.

What does Dig co-creator Mike Schur think of Parks and Recreation comparisons?

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Right from the moment Peacock's Dig was announced, comparisons to Parks and Recreation felt inevitable.

The new sitcom reunites Parks and Recreation's co-creator Mike Schur with the show's leading lady, Amy Poehler. As co-creators on Dig, the two once more deliver a workplace comedy about a competent, passionate leader, played by Poehler, who assembles a motley team. In Dig's case, that's archaeologist Elise, who's overseeing a Grecian archaeological dig site.

Comparison to a prior project is territory Schur and Poehler know well, dating back to the first days of Parks and Recreation.

"When we started Parks and Rec, we were compared to The Office, because The Office was such a big deal at the time," Schur told Mashable over Zoom. "That was sort of our mini obstacle we had to get through."

Both shows were workplace-set mockumentaries on NBC. Critics and viewers alike were quick to call out the similarities.

While talking with Steve Carell for an episode of Good Hang earlier this year, Poehler recalled, "Parks and Rec had the worst launch ever. Everyone was like, 'This is not The Office. We don't like this.'"

However, Parks and Recreation ditched Office-like cynicism in favor of a warmer tone, and it carved out a niche of its own. Still, for Schur, comparisons to The Office prepared him for how people might react to his later work. That includes Dig.

"[With Dig,] we're being compared to a different show that we also made," Schur said. "We're experienced with the concept that people have expectations for something going in. And we're a little bit older and wiser in terms of how we manage that emotionally."

While some might say that comparison is the thief of joy, Schur has found the upside.

"I think it's a position of privilege to be in, to have a show that people are like, 'I hope it's like this other thing you did,'" Schur said. "That's great, because that means people watched the first one and cared about it. So I hope that people will watch this new show and judge it on its own merits."

Dig pulls off a perfect Parks and Rec reunion

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Peacock's Dig is just one Parks and Recreation reunion after another.

The archaeological comedy is co-created by Amy Poehler and Mike Schur, who both worked together on Parks and Recreation. Schur co-created that series with Greg Daniels, and Poehler led the show as waffle-loving civil servant Leslie Knope.

However, Poehler and Schur's collaboration isn't the only spark of Parks and Recreation magic you'll find in Dig. In a blink-and-you'll-miss-it moment, Dig also features a cameo from none other than Rashida Jones, who plays Leslie's best friend, nurse Ann Perkins, in Parks and Recreation.

Jones' appearance occurs in episode 5, when she pops up as a woman taking a photo of archaeologists Elise (Poehler) and Neville (Hugh Laurie) at a beachside restaurant. My Parks-loving self has never rewound a scene so fast to make sure my eyes weren't deceiving me.

According to Schur, the cameo came about due to proximity. Dig shot in Italy and Greece, and Jones is semi-permanently located in London. Schur, Poehler, and Jones, who have been friends since even before working together on Parks and Recreation, were communicating with one another during production.

"We were just texting with [Jones], and we were like, 'It's not that long a flight. What if you just hopped over here and hung out with us at this hotel we're staying at?'" Schur told Mashable over Zoom. "Then once she was there, it was a very quick jump to, 'You've got to just go into the show. You've got to pop in for a scene.'"

From there, Schur, Poehler, and Jones came up with the quick photo cameo.

"That's part of the fun of knowing people and working together for a really long time, is you do silly things like that," Schur recalled. "It was really fun, and she was totally game for it."

The highlight of the scene comes right after Jones exits, when Elise casually remarks, "She's very pretty."

For Parks and Recreation fans, the compliment instantly recalls Leslie's non-stop raves over Ann's beauty, whether it's calling her a "beautiful tropical fish" or a "poetic, noble land-mermaid." It's a sweet reminder of one of TV's most enduring female friendships, and it's right at home in Dig, which is full of sweet female friendships in its own right. Plus, according to Schur, the remark was completely improvised by Poehler.

"It was a very smart little mini gift to the folks who were fans of that show who will enjoy seeing them back together again," Schur said.

The Best Stocking Stuffers Are Small but Special—Here Are 34 Ideas We Love

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

When we’re shopping for the holidays, we start by thinking about what’s under the tree. But sometimes the coolest gifts are the ones that end up in your stocking. Just because they’re small doesn’t mean they’re not special. That’s why our shopping team set out to find the best stocking stuffer ideas, from the pretty to the practical (and everything in between).

Some families may choose to drop in small gifts every day leading up to Christmas, and some may just hang up stockings as part of their holiday decor—but not fill them up. Regardless of your approach, everyone knows the best stocking stuffers should be small, thoughtful, useful, and not overly expensive. Think of a candle snuffer, a refreshing room mist, or a bottle stopper that keeps their favorite bottle of wine fresh.

We always recommend personalizing each stocking with thoughtful touches tailored to the recipient. Consider what they love to collect, how they spend their free time, or something they’ve mentioned wanting in recent conversations. For homebodies, think cozy socks or candle holders. The 5 Most Unwatchable James Bond Movies, Ranked

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The James Bond franchise has long stood as one of cinema’s most defining blockbuster titles. From the outset, way back when it started with Sean Connery in the lead role in 1962’s Dr. No, it has epitomized the suave spectacle and sophisticated charm of high-octane spy thrills. Ranging from Goldfinger to Skyfall, the saga has delivered some of the most exhilarating spy-action movies ever made, but any franchise that features 25 movies is bound to have a few misfires and, unfortunately, 007 is no different.

When the franchise is at its worst, it reduces its tropes to being crude caricatures of the genre. The gadgets become goofy, the Bond girl an affront to any form of progressive femininity, and the story often suffers most under the strain of forced stylistic choices, laughably cartoonish action sequences, and a sense of antagonism that is utterly ridiculous. Ranging from early errs that were painfully anachronistic even when they were released to more modern missteps, these blundering Bond movies represent cinema’s greatest franchise at its unwatchable worst.

Released 12 years after Connery's last Bond outing—1971’s Diamonds Are Forever—and marking just the second and latest non-Eon-produced Bond movie ever made, Never Say Never Again emerged amid a time of chaos and calamity for the franchise. Roger Moore’s ongoing tenure as the official 007 had begun to falter, and while his worst movies mark some of the franchise’s lowest points, they are made watchable through a sense of characterful campiness and, in some cases, a certain so-bad-it’s-good appeal. Neither of those attributes can be applied to Never Say Never Again, which sees a 53-year-old Connery return to the role to deliver what is effectively a tame rehash of Thunderball.

The lack of originality in the picture is only worsened by the fundamental Bond staples it was deprived of. Given it wasn’t an Eon Productions project, it never had access to the iconic Bond theme, the opening gun-barrel shot, or John Barry’s signature score. Without these qualities, the film lands with a certain flatness. It lacks authentic action-thriller energy, and its efforts to at least modernize the character by implementing ‘80s trends like arcade-style dueling only make it all the more outdated today. It isn’t the worst or most egregious Bond movie ever made, but it is among the most banal, and that pervasive sense of muted mediocrity makes it an unwatchable snoozefest in 2026.

Quantum of Solace is as bland and banal a Bond movie as has ever been made. The follow-up to Daniel Craig’s dashing debut in Casino Royale undoubtedly fell victim to the 2007/08 writer’s strike in Hollywood, making it indistinguishable from any run-of-the-mill, B-grade spy thriller. It lacks the ruthless hard-edged grunt of its predecessor, the snappy stylistic flourishes of its successor, and refuses to implement anything in the way of camp charm or thematic prowess. Even the villain’s environmental slant is ill-explored, making him an odd and forgettable concoction of timely ideals and traditional egomaniacal Bond villainy.

It is ultimately a Bond movie that lacks an identity. It doesn’t strive to be anything unique, refusing to stretch beyond the safe limitations of the franchise at its most middling and mediocre. To say it is an outright terrible film would perhaps be going too far, it just stands as a flick that has no aspiration of what it wants to be. Again, the writer’s strike of the time was definitely a limiting factor on what Quantum of Solace could achieve, but for a Bond movie to be plain boring is unforgivable. Even many of the worst installments of the franchise have their goofy eccentricities that at least give them something, but Quantum of Solace lacks anything memorable, and it derides viewers of any reason to want to rewatch it.

Most, if not all, of the original Connery Bond films have elements that haven’t aged all too well. In the best of them, it’s easily dismissed as a sign of the times, but in the lesser entries of his tenure as 007, it’s a bit more grating to watch. His fifth outing, You Only Live Twice, is the epitome of this notion. It lacks the pizzazz and pop of Connery’s earlier, more iconic movies, and it seems as though Connery himself knows it more than anyone. His performance is uncharacteristically wooden, a stilted cutout of a character where his charisma and quick-wit ought to be.

Bereft of Connery’s best work, the film struggles to apply wallpaper over the narrative cracks and biting insensitivities. Roald Dahl’s screenplay leans heavily on racial stereotypes—an idea that reaches an ugly crescendo with Bond’s infamous “yellowface” disguise as a Japanese man—and his endeavor to up the ante comes at an emotional cost. The movie is a big, drab blockbuster with no feeling, powered by senseless villainy and over-the-top theatrics. Upon release, You Only Live Once was a limp entry to the franchise, but today it is a bitterly hard-to-watch mishit that serves as a painstaking procession of every nasty trope and tendency the original Bond pictures had.

2006’s Casino Royale is a true masterpiece of action cinema, slick sophistication, and gripping high-stakes drama that finds the perfect balance between exhilarating set piece and character-charged intensity. The 1967 interpretation of the Bond story, however, is not. The unlicensed Bond adaptation follows a retired 007 (David Niven) as he is forced to re-enter the field to bring down the evil international crime faction SMERSH, which has been behind a recent series of deadly attacks on MI6 operatives. In an effort to mislead the criminal syndicate, several others also pose as agent 007, including baccarat master Evelyn Tremble (Peter Sellers) and Bond’s own former secretary, Miss Moneypenny (Barbara Bouchet).

The idea of lampooning the spy genre was good in theory, but the execution of 1967’s Casino Royale leaves much to be desired. Its story is a chaotic mess that is completely incomprehensible and never comes close to filling the film’s bloated 131-minute runtime, while on-set conflicts saw the movie pulled in several different directions, making the final product intangible. Even a supporting cast including Orson Welles, Woody Allan, and original Bond girl Ursula Andress are incapable of elevating the film beyond its pitiful nature. It was admonished by the masses upon release, and it has only gotten worse over the years.

The Bond franchise has had its fair share of mishits over the decades, but none have been quite so disastrous and agonizing as 2002’s Die Another Day. It marked an unfortunate end to Pierce Brosnan’s tenure as 007, which started out so well with GoldenEye giving a clear intent of suave style and gritty action. Three films later, the Bond saga had regressed to uninspiring uncertainty. Die Another Day is an awkward, disjointed conglomerate of the litany of Bond styles that came before it, and every tonal shift and plot twist it aspires for falls flat on its face.

The opening sequence has a certain gruffness to it, with Bond being tortured by the North Korean army over the course of 14 months, but whatever intrigue that hard-edged intro may have had immediately dissipates as the movie pivots to cringe-worthy campiness. Not only is Die Another Day’s narrative tempo off beat, its reliance on appalling CGI and ridiculous concepts to carry some of its action sequences is enough to leave viewers in hysterics. It marks an undisputed low-point for the franchise, a painful depiction of 007 at its absolute worst. The only saving grace of the movie is that it was so bad it forced producers to take an entirely new approach four years later when the saga was re-ignited with Casino Royale.

Everything We Know About Spider-Man & Wolverine Being In Avengers: Doomsday

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

It's almost time for Avengers: Doomsday, and rumors swirl that Spider-Man and Wolverine will turn up. Marvel is determined to one-up even Avengers: Endgame, with this movie bringing back heroes from the last 26 years of Marvel blockbusters. The final Doomsday trailer divides its time between Steve Rogers and the X-Men, and even marked a franchise first: we finally got James Marsden's Cyclops saying an iconic line, "To me, my X-Men."

The X-Men, the Fantastic Four, and several different teams of Avengers are all going up against Robert Downey, Jr.'s Doctor Doom. But with the entire multiverse expected to end ahead of next year's Avengers: Secret Wars, we're all pretty certain there are going to be a lot more surprises. There have been particularly exciting rumors about both Spider-Man and Wolverine, but how likely are they to be true?

There's currently no evidence Tom Holland's Spider-Man is in Avengers: Doomsday. The core issue lies in the real-world, with the fact there are only small gaps in Holland's schedule where he could have been available for filming; he was occupied on The Odyssey through most of Doomsday's production, before moving on to Spider-Man: Brand New Day. If he does make an appearance at all, it would have to be a very small one indeed.

That said, there's intense debate about whether the end of Spider-Man: Brand New Day is actually Doomsday setup. It shows Ned Leeds' "Spider-Tracker" app losing Spider-Man completely, only to spot him in space. This is most likely a multiversal collision known as an incursion, where two different Earths collide. It's possible Holland's Spider-Man has been transported to another Earth, one that's about to impact Earth-616.

Tobey Maguire and Andrew Garfield both reprised their roles in Spider-Man: No Way Home, meaning they've already been part of the Multiverse Saga. It wouldn't be at all surprising to see them turn up; in fact, there have been rumors Doomsday kicks off with Maguire's Spider-Man killed in battle with the X-Men, who are fighting to save their universe. We'll have to wait and see whether that actually happens.

One thing's for sure: If Maguire or Garfield do return as Spider-Man in Avengers: Doomsday, it's likely to only be for a small role. The film principally involves three different Earths: Earth-616, the Fantastic Four's world, and the X-Men's. That already means Doomsday has a lot of cast to juggle (even more, given Doctor Doom may have been collecting people from other timelines as well). Many characters are likely to be relegated to cameos, just to make the story navigable.

Marvel is keeping very quiet about whether Hugh Jackman's Wolverine is in Doomsday. That said, the House of Ideas do seem intent on keeping Jackman on board till he's 90 (at least), and Deadpool and Wolverine brought the character into the Multiverse Saga. In this case, it feels likely Wolverine is being kept back as a surprise. But nobody will be shocked should Logan pop his claws and fight alongside the rest of the X-Men. Perhaps he's even one of the heroes who responds to Cyclops' call to arms in the trailer.

Speaking as a lifelong X-Men fan, I'm delighted to see other members of the team get the spotlight in Doomsday's marketing. Fox depended far too much on Wolverine's popularity, and it's been so good to see Marvel course-correct a little, choosing to emphasize the likes of Professor X and Cyclops. I'm sure there are going to be more X-Men surprises, too.

Oddly enough, though, there are actually quite a few heroes couldn't missing from Avengers: Doomsday's roll call. Sticking with the X-Men, two characters stand out: Ryan Reynold's Deadpool and Famke Janssen's Jean Grey. The former has already appeared in the Multiverse Saga, and even has connections to the Time Variance Authority. The latter is an absolute powerhouse (literally the Phoenix), so there's likely a story reason here. Perhaps Jean has already been killed (or recruited) by Doctor Doom.

Far too many heroes from Earth-616 are still absent. Brie Larson's Captain Marvel is surprising, again because she's one of the most powerful heroes in play. A multiversal event really should feature the "boss of space," so we can only hope she's going to turn up. Teyonah Parris' Spectrum was stranded in the X-Men timeline at the end of The Marvels, which should surely mean she's unwittingly triggering an incursion between Earth-616 and the X-Men universe. It would actually be rather odd for Marvel to ignore her in Doomsday.

The problem, of course, is that the MCU expanded a little too quickly during the Multiverse Saga. We were introduced to so many different superheroes; some are street level (such as Alaqua Cox's Echo), while others didn't prove especially popular (the less said about Secret Invasion's new Super-Skrulls, the better). Avengers: Doomsday already faces a huge challenge if it's to give due time to multiple Avengers teams as well as the X-Men, so some Earth-616 heroes are going to be neglected. Don't expect to see the Eternals anytime soon.

That said, Marvel did recently confirm two new heroes for Doomsday. Hailee Steinfeld is returning as Kate Bishop, meaning at least one of Marvel's two Hawkeyes (and, in this case, a Young Avenger in the comics) is due to appear. Meanwhile, VisionQuest's post-credits scene confirmed Doctor Doom has resurrected Elizabeth Olsen's Scarlet Witch, presumably confirming rumors Wanda Maximoff is one of the Latverian Witches we see in the recent trailer. If these two heroes are also back, then it's possible Marvel has quite a few surprises in the works. We'll find out more when Avengers: Doomsday finally releases in December.

What you just touched can change how the next thing feels

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

A study in rats found that frontal brain activity helped predict how an earlier vibration would bias judgments about the next one.

What you feel right now depends partly on what you felt a moment ago. In rats trained to judge vibrations, the primary sensory cortex mainly tracked the current vibration.

A region of the frontal cortex represented both the current and previous vibrations, and its activity helped predict how the previous vibration biased the rats’ judgments.

The study was carried out by neuroscientists at SISSA, the International School for Advanced Studies in Trieste, Italy.

“Perception is often thought of as a direct mapping of the external world,” explained Mathew Diamond, a cognitive neuroscientist at SISSA.

“Yet what we experience depends not only on what is happening now, but also on what happened moments ago. In short, every percept is constructed against a backdrop of recent experience.”

Scientists call this effect serial dependence, and it’s easy to picture. “Imagine feeling a vibration with your fingertip (for instance, from the cell phone) and then feeling a second vibration shortly afterward,” Diamond said.

“The second stimulus will not be experienced as an independent event; instead, perception is systematically biased by prior inputs. The brain, in effect, carries traces of the recent past into the present.”

“Perception is not a photograph of the world. It is more like a film, in which the experience of each frame is influenced by those that came before.”

Psychologists have known about the effect for a long time. Where the brain does the mixing, and how, has been much harder to work out.

Theories of “predictive processing” propose that the brain constantly combines incoming signals with expectations built from experience. What nobody had clearly shown was the neural machinery that brings the two together.

To find it, Diamond’s team trained rodents on a simple touch task. On each trial the animal felt a stimulus and had to decide whether it was weak or strong.

Meanwhile, the researchers recorded the activity of hundreds of neurons.

The animals’ judgments were consistently pulled by the previous trial. After a strong stimulus, the next one was more likely to be judged weak.

After a weak one, the next was more likely to be judged strong. A medium-strength touch could feel faint or firm depending on what had come right before it.

“The key finding emerged from comparing two cortical regions,” said lead author Iacopo Hachen.

“Activity in the primary sensory cortex mainly reflected the stimulus currently being processed, a direct representation of the ongoing input.”

“By contrast, neurons in a frontal cortical area carried information about both the current stimulus and recent sensory history. There, neural representations of the present were embedded within a context established by the past.”

The first stop for touch signals in the cortex, in other words, stayed true to the present. The frontal region carried the present and the recent past together.

The frontal region also helped predict the animals’ choices.

When its activity rose or fell from one trial to the next, the animals’ judgments shifted in step.

“Variations in this frontal-cortex activity predicted corresponding variations in perceptual judgments from one trial to the next, suggesting that while the sensory cortex faithfully reports the ongoing input, the frontal cortex integrates that input with recent experience to shape perception,” Hachen explained.

One region reports what is happening, and the other decides what it means given what just happened.

The researchers also identified a possible mechanism at the level of individual cells.

Fast-spiking neurons played a prominent role in how the frontal region represented both vibrations. Their firing patterns suggest they may be inhibitory neurons.

There was also an unexpected finding. While a touch was happening, the frontal neurons encoded it as an ongoing sensory event.

Once the animal committed to its answer, the code switched to a different, memory-like format. That stored version lasted and went on to bias the next decision.

In other words, the brain doesn’t just keep a recording of what happened. It rewrites the experience into a new format as it moves from perception into memory.

That rewritten trace then colors the next moment.

The authors see the work as evidence for a basic principle of how the brain works. Perception comes from a constant back-and-forth between new sensory evidence and traces of the recent past.

“Our findings suggest that the brain does not treat each moment in isolation. Instead, the recent past becomes part of the neural representation of the present, a unitary construct that mirrors our experience,” Hachen concluded.

Understanding this exchange could help explain how prediction normally supports behavior, and how it might go wrong in neurological and psychiatric disorders.

The study is published in the journal Science Advances.

Markiplier’s Iron Lung Officially Returns In 3 Weeks With Exciting New Horror Release

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Markiplier's Iron Lung is about to terrorize viewers once again.

Based on David Szymanski's hit viral video game of the same name, the horror film centers on Simon, a convict living in a distant future in which all stars, inhabited planets and most of humanity have vanished, and is tasked with exploring a desolate moon with an ocean of blood in a small submarine. Written, directed by and starring Markiplier, Iron Lung left critics somewhat divided but was a thorough smash with audiences and at the box office.

Now, nearly a year after the film hit theaters, Markiplier has offered a new update on his Iron Lung adaptation, revealing that it is officially getting a DVD and Blu-ray release. The horror hit, which is currently available for pre-order in both of its versions, is slated to release in three weeks, with the filmmaker indicating it will arrive around December 13, when he will also host a special livestream offering a complete behind-the-scenes look at the creation of the film and its unanswered questions.

In addition to the film itself, the Iron Lung Blu-ray and DVDs, which cost $30 and $20 respectively, are said to come with over three hours of never-before-seen bonus content, including a behind-the-scenes documentary entitled One with the Blood running just over two hours long, as well as various other featurettes and yet-to-be-announced content.

The long wait for an Iron Lung physical release is sure to have been a surprising one compared to the general three-to-four-month turnaround for other films. However, as Markiplier explains in the video above, the reason for this stemmed from a desire to prevent losing creative control over his hit movie, and the desire to give an in-depth Blu-ray experience.

It certainly falls in line with the way the filmmaker treated Iron Lung throughout its initial development, which was in and of itself a slow-but-steady process. After being announced in Spring 2023, production was hit with a few notable setbacks, including the 2023 SAG-AFTRA strike, as well as dealing with minor injuries from the fake blood used on set, which set a new world record with over 80,000 gallons used. Moreover, the film took nearly two years from when he finished its post-production to ultimately giving it a grassroots-like independent theatrical release.

Ultimately, Markiplier's efforts have frequently proven worthwhile for Iron Lung. Despite coming out in January, an infamously lackluster window at the box office, the movie saw its opening weekend projections continually increase as anticipation grew around it, with it ultimately opening to $18.2 million, well surpassing its production budget, which the filmmaker has estimated being between $4–5 million. By the end of its run, it had grossed $51.2 million, somewhere between 10-13 times its budget.

While not as talked-about as its successors, Markiplier's Iron Lung notably kicked off 2026 as the year of YouTubers-turned-filmmakers taking over the box office. The combined might of Curry Barker's Obsession and Kane Parsons' Backrooms, which together grossed close to $925 million, has seen Hollywood now turning to other similar stories and projects, while also pushing forward with new titles from both Barker and Parsons.

Obsession has become the latest in a long line of horror films made by YouTube creators, and it's just one of six films that shows their creativity.

Now that Iron Lung is slated for a physical media release, it will be interesting to see how much its previous success carries over into a medium that many studios have started to phase out. Considering the passionate fanbase Markiplier and the film have, and it being a limited-run release, there's a very real chance it could enjoy a similar level of success and, depending on the speed of its inventory sales, may even get a restocking at some point in the future.

Iron Lung is a film set in a post-apocalyptic future where a convict is tasked with exploring a blood ocean on a desolate moon. Utilizing a small submarine called the Iron Lung, he navigates the aftermath of the Quiet Rapture, which caused the disappearance of stars and habitable planets.

What To Buy With Gift Cards: 28 Best Things To Spend Your Holiday Money On

Because nobody else is going to buy you that weighted vest you’ve been eyeballing for months.

And listen, don’t do that thing we all sometimes do and leave them to languish in a desk drawer or a dusty corner of your inbox. January is the perfect time to treat yourself to the stuff you’d never splurge on otherwise. Buy the fancy version of something you use every day! Hit Ulta to refill your empty beauty bottles! Put that Visa gift card toward something that will make you feel better!

Here’s what the Scary Mommy team is spending our holiday gift cards on this year, and we hope you’ll find some inspiration here for your own post-holidays shopping spree.

I love the concept behind this knife: A female chef was tired of all kitchen knives being designed and fitted for men, so she literally took matters into her own hands. Enter the Cardinal knife, which comes with multiple handles for the size of your grip. This is such a great, high-quality knife, and having it fit my hand makes chopping so much easier and more enjoyable. I love the red color, too. And like everything I buy these days, it's dishwasher-safe. — Sarah Aswell, Deputy Editor, News & Social

I love to use holiday gift cards to zhoosh up my normal routine by spending a little more on “everyday” stuff, like fancy toothpaste. Before Oral Care’s whitening toothpaste uses nano-hydroxyapatite, a mineral ingredient that’s having a major moment. Plus, it skips the peroxide, so it’s gentler than the strips that leave my teeth zinging. I also really appreciate that it comes in a recyclable, plant-based tube. — Julie Sprankles, Deputy Editor, Lifestyle & Entertainment

I feel like I am constantly looking for a flashlight in my house, and when I finally find one, it's out of batteries or not bright enough to help me. This rechargeable flashlight has solved all of my problems. It is incredibly bright, it can either be plugged in or used with traditional batteries, and it is substantial enough to beat an intruder to death with it if it comes to that. I have it in one safe, special place where I know I can grab it quickly when I need it. — Sarah Aswell

If you want to spend a gift card on something you’ll use every single night, a pillow is the way to go, and this one has a curved shape that’s designed to cradle your neck and shoulders whether you sleep on your side or your back. The fill is a copper-infused, CertiPUR-US certified foam that stays cool, plus there’s a hidden zipper so you can add or remove fill until it’s exactly the right height, and it comes with a cooling organic bamboo pillowcase. It’s handmade in California, comes in queen, king, and travel sizes, and has a 60-night trial, so it’s a low-risk splurge. — Katie Garrity, Senior Editor, News & Social

David Greaves Says ‘Once Upon a Time in Harlem’ Is “An Act of Resistance” Against Efforts to Erase Black History

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

When William Greaves died before completing his Harlem Renaissance documentary, his son David, who had helped to shoot it but had never directed a feature film of his own, stepped in to finish it: "I couldn't imagine anybody else doing it," he tells The Hollywood Reporter.

When the veteran newspaperman David Greaves told people about the film that his father, the legendary documentarian William Greaves, had been trying to complete for decades — the one built around footage of a 1972 reunion of Harlem Renaissance luminaries at Duke Ellington’s apartment — some asked him why anyone would want to watch a bunch of old people sitting around talking. He bit his tongue at the time, but recalled during an interview at the recent SCAD Savannah Film Festival, “I was thinking, ‘Oh, yeah, watch this!’ Because I knew what those old people were talking about, and how important it was.” Related Stories 'Awards Chatter' Podcast Danny Boyle on 'Ink' and Murdoch, the 'Trainspotting' Toilet Scene and How 'Slumdog Millionaire' Almost Went Straight to Video SCAD Savannah Film Festival 'American Doctor' Director on Her Powerful Gaza Doc: "I Wanted to Do Something"

David Greaves has been proven correct by the tremendous reception of that documentary, a one-of-a-kind chronicle of a critical chapter in African-American history, which finally premiered at the Sundance Film Festival in January, screened at the Cannes Film Festival in May back and was released by Neon in October under the title Once Upon a Time in Harlem. Sadly, William Greaves didn’t live to see the film’s completion — he died in 2014 — but his work was finished by his son, who had been a 26-year-old cameraman at the 1972 gathering, but had never directed a film before. “I couldn’t imagine anybody else doing it, frankly,” David explained during a post-screening Q&A at Savannah’s Lucas Theatre. (He also was one of 10 directors of outstanding 2026 docs who appeared on the fest’s Doc to Watch Panel.)

The fact that it took more than a half-century for the film to come to fruition was largely the result of the perfectionist tendencies of William and his second wife, Louise, David’s stepmother, who died in 2023. “They had continued shooting on through the decades,” further tracing the history of the Harlem Renaissance, David reports. While he’s sorry that his father and stepmother weren’t able to see the enthusiastic response to the film from audiences of all ages and races, he also feels that it all happened for a reason: “I think that this film was made to come out now, at a very important time in American history, when [some in positions of power] want to erase Black history,” he asserted. “This film is sort of an act of resistance against that.”

Anya Taylor-Joy’s Brutal Revenge Fantasy Officially Returns

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Lade Omotade is a News and Features Author at Collider, covering everything from casting announcements and streaming trends to behind-the-scenes stories and the cultural impact of today's biggest film and TV franchises. Since joining the team in 2024, she has written more than 1,000 news stories, with her work also cited by outlets like AOL and IMDb.

The holiday season is officially right around the corner, with Thanksgiving this Thursday and Christmas only a few weeks away. As the end-of-year festivities kick into high gear, there is no better time to unwind and catch up on your watchlist from the comfort of home—and free streaming has just the perfect collection. From heartwarming holiday classics to blockbuster movies and binge-worthy new series, you can make the most of the upcoming break with the best free streaming offers, including a new addition: Anya Taylor-Joy's fantasy masterpiece, which boasts a 90% Rotten Tomatoes score.

Best known for playing chess prodigy Beth Harmon in the Netflix miniseries The Queen's Gambit, Taylor-Joy has gone on to build a versatile career spanning thriller, fantasy, comedy, action, and science fiction. One of her most addictive fantasy works is now much easier to watch, arriving on the video-on-demand service Fandango ahead of Christmas and after Thanksgiving, TV Insider can exclusively reveal.

Starting December 3, you can stream The Northman for free on Fandango. This comes four years after its theatrical release, during which it earned rave reviews from critics and found success on VOD, though it underperformed at the box office. The Norse film joins a lineup of familiar holiday favorites arriving on the platform in December, including the 1994 classic Miracle on 34th Street, A Christmas Carol starring George C. Scott, The Family Stone, The Fabelmans, and The Passion of the Christ.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

Starring Alexander Skarsgård as Viking warrior prince Amleth, The Northman follows his vengeful journey and explores how far he will go to avenge his father's murder, King Aurvandill "War-Raven" (Ethan Hawke). The king was apparently beheaded by his brother, Fjölnir (Claes Bang), who staged a palace coup. Amleth narrowly escapes Fjölnir's assassins by fleeing by boat, vowing to kill Fjölnir and rescue his mother, Queen Gudrún (Nicole Kidman). Along his quest for revenge, Amleth meets Olga (Taylor-Joy), a sorceress who shares his goal of defeating Fjölnir.

Directed by Robert Eggers, who co-wrote the screenplay with Sjón, The Northman is among the heaviest revenge movies of all time according to a recent Collider ranking. It is also considered one of the greatest epic fantasy movies, with critics consistently praising its visuals, Eggers's visionary direction, and the central cast's brilliant performances. Similarly, in Collider's review, Ross Bonaime gave the film a B+, commending Eggers's leap to a large-scale epic with a more expansive storyline while maintaining his distinctive style and substance.

The Northman arrives on Fandango next week.

Seriously, Lionsgate resuscitated life back into this YA series after it hit an all-time low opening and result three years with The Hunger Games: Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes ($44.6M domestic opening, $166.3M North American take, $363.9M worldwide). Momentous 61st Venice Biennale Ends With 2nd-Highest Visitor Turnout

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Political disputes, a historic strike, and the curator's sudden passing did not keep visitors away.

The 61st Venice Biennale, curated by the late Koyo Kouoh, concluded yesterday, November 22, reporting 714,015 visitors during its six-month run. Nearly 30,000 visitors attended the pre-opening of In Minor Keys, which included a record-high 100 participating countries.

Despite running three weeks shorter than previous editions to allow for the installation of the renovated Central Pavilion at the Giardini, In Minor Keys is the second highest-attended exhibition in the event's history. (The 2022 edition holds the record, counting more than 800,000 visitors.) The news follows the highest-ever visitor numbers at the most recent editions of the Venice Architecture Biennale and Venice International Film Festival, two other landmark events organized under the La Biennale di Venezia.

Following Kouoh's unprecedented death in May 2025 at the age of 57, her team of collaborators — Rasha Salti, Marie Hélène Pereira, Gabe Beckhurst Feijoo, Siddhartha Mitter, and Rory Tsapayi — carried out the exhibition of 109 international artists, many of whom hailed from Africa and the Caribbean diasporas.

New Mexico is the reward

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The library is a bit chaotic, all the books are in the process of being boxed up for a move. I ask when they are moving and where to. The librarian doesn’t know herself. There are two computers there which Brokeback and i jump on to fill out our NDA permit forms. There isn’t any clear guidance on how to fill them out, so we call Fort Huachua and ask some questions. The person who answers say i need to have an active service member, veteran or DOD contractor as a sponsor. This doesn’t seem to add up to what the CDTC says about international hikers, we just need to be with a US citizen who also had the permit. So i skip this on the form. We go into a back room with whitish walls to take pictures  for the permit. Mine is pretty bad! I compile all the documents and send everything off to the Fort Huachuca email. Fingers crossed!

I wanted to do some blogging but turns out the library closes for lunch. Brokeback and i walk back into town. Chama is a pretty long town that stretches along the road, so its a while. I drop into the grocery store to get more of my resupply. There i meet Tim & Cassie again. They are staying with JJ and invite me to come over for a dinner they are cooking. I say yes, and after finishing we walk back into town. We spend the afternoon hanging out with the rest of the Oysters outside the Iron Rail Inn. Later we walk over to JJ’s with Tim & Cassie. There we make dinner together and chat for a good while. I also get to peek around the Exoticpax  and Aardwolf production rooms. Its pretty cool getting to see their setups as someone who also makes gear.

In true CDT fashion its covered in barbed wire and bones!

Groves of golden Aspen were very common on this part of the trail

Its pretty cold overnight, and at about 5am I’m awake. There’s frost on the ground and so after heading off to dig my morning cathole, i decide to pack up and start hiking in the dark.  I reach the edge of the mesa we are on shortly after dawn and am treated to views of the pine forests below.

Looking down towards Abiquiu in the morning

After this the trail descends down towards Ghost Ranch and pretty quickly the landscape changes from pine forest to something more arid. Its when we reach the Arroyo del Yeso that we get our first true sense of being in the desert!

Our first true view of the New Mexico desert near Ghost Ranch

Every September Albuquerque hosts the hot air balloon fiesta. Back in Chama we decided to go if we could get tickets. Its a couple of hours drive, through a desert landscape. I look around as we drive and wonder if this is what the rest of New Mexico is going to be like.

We get tickets for the morning session, so wake up at about 4am the next day. I feel pretty awful on the short car ride over. By the time we are walking into the Arena i start to feel pretty excited, and after getting coffee i feel pretty good.  I imagined we would be in a viewing area away from the balloon. However you get to walk in among them as they set up which was so cool! The first balloons lifted off just before sunrise to the strains of the US National Anthem. After this its was a constant stream of balloons being inflated and lifting off! There must have been around 100 balloons in the air at one point.  2027 SOBO’s, this is one amazing sidequest!

The first balloons lifting off before dawn

After leaving the festival we got breakfast and then napped back at the hotel. Did some laundry and just hung out for the rest of the day. Later we drove to an all you can eat sushi restaurant and demolished many rolls!

While visiting Albuqerque had been fun, it was definitely a little overwhelming to be in such a big city. So i was glad to be heading back to trail. Tengram dropped us off at Ghost Ranch around midday. He was going to camp with us that evening so we put camping bits in his car and get slacked again.

The crew hiking out towards the Rio Chama

The route out of Ghost Ranch is pretty nice, and goes on a piece of the land that you usually don’t get to access. Now there were big sandstone cliffs in almost all directions and it felt like we were truly in the desert. We crossed the highway and followed the dirt road up alongside the Rio Chama towards where the Ghost Ranch Alt rejoins the redline. Halfway along there is a campsite where Tengram is setup. We gather for dinner and then the projector comes out for the Second Twilight Movie! I enjoy it in a its so bad its good kind of way.

The others in the Oysters were going to do a backyard ultra to Cuba the next day, about 50 miles. I decided that this wasn’t really for me, i always prefer a more even split of days over doing an epic one and zeroing to recover. So after the film had finished i set off. It started to rain pretty soon after i set off. At first a little bit but gets pretty heavy. Walking down the dirt road in the dark and rain makes me wonder if i should have stayed and camped with the others. In the end i hike for an hour or so to rejoin the CDT redline and make a little progress. By the time i find somewhere to camp the rain has stopped. There is a little tree with some pine duff which isn’t too damp.

I’m woken up before by the Oysters passing as they start their ultra. While i’m not planning on hiking 50 miles, i still need to do a pretty big day if i want to catch up with them in Cuba. So i pack up and set off in the dark. The sunrise over the cliffs is pretty nice and the trail is kind of chill for a bit.

It follows a small stream in a shallow canyon before climbing up to a mesa. Here the trail has really been washed out by the heavy rain. The water has scoured out the streambed a good 4 or 5 feet in places, and there are areas where flood debris has been jammed up against trees. The result is a constant climbing in and out of the streambed trying to follow the trail. Its pretty tiring and I’m almost glad when i leave and get to climb up onto the Mesa.

From there the trail continues up high before dropping down towards a highway. Looking out over the landscape i realize that before i know it this journey will be over. I have a little bittersweet cry on my way down.

One thing this trail does is constantly surprise me with the landscapes. In my head New Mexico is an entire state like The Great Divide Basin.  Climbing up into The San Luis Parks Wilderness area it feels a little bit like I’m back in Colorado. There are loads of aspens and wide open meadowy spaces. I stop around sunset for dinner, then keep hiking into the dark. Its a little spooky, especially when i see hunters our with their headtorches on. I also go the wrong way for about a mile and have to turn back.

Eventually i  pass the San Gregoria Reservoir and come to the southern boundary of the Wilderness area. Here there is a trailhead with pit toilet, where i find a flattish spot to camp.  Im pretty tired and Its only another 20km into Cuba, which is all downhill and i should be able to easily do in the morning.

In the morning i set off from the trailhead and start descending. There are some cow paths and i end up paralleling the CDT for a while, and have to do a little bushwhack back to the path. As the trail descends the trees open up, the groundcover gets sparser and it feels more arid.  Soon the trail leaves the trees and comes to a little overlook. From here i can see out to Cuba. Now it feels like i am truly in the desert!

The rest of the way into Cuba is along dirt roads. Cuba is pretty notorious for loose dogs that sometimes come out and bite hikers. There are plenty of tales of hikers having to get stitches from bites. I’ll admit that dogs scare me a little bit, and so i pretty alert on my way in. Close to town there was a house with a couple of dogs outside, who barked at me, but otherwise stayed put.

In Cuba i reunite with the Oysters at one of the motels there around lunchtime. I do my town chores while we watch Tombstone and drink Coors Banquets. Later we head down to a cafe to get dinner. While we are eating a man comes over. His name is Jim and he does trail maintenance on the CDT around the area, and talks to us about our hike. Then says that he’s settled up the bill for us! A nice and much welcomed surprise.

This first week in New Mexico has been such a nice change in the nature of the hike. Without the need to push big days hanging over me, I feel like I can relax and really enjoy this last part of the hike.

1. The attack is only a concern for functionality not protected by a pin, or in the case of OATH OTP (including TOTP), by a password.

2. The attack only matters if the original Yubikey would be returned to the owner after cloning. Otherwise, the attacker would just try to use the original.

I think this particular attack primarily would be of concern in enterprise applications— an employee could try to clone their employer-issued key before returning it to the employer at severance, for instance.
The description of how the hack is implemented is to expose the circuit board so you can then use a very specializied equipment. This would be a Mission Impossible movie type situation where an covert agency with a lot of resource implant an agent. This is not possible by a run of the mill person.

I would imagine if a person ends employment, the employer should disable all account and device access.
