Buck & Eddie’s 9-1-1 Fakeout Reconciliation Spells A Bigger Problem In Season 10

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

When Buck took over caring for his biological son, Theo, in 9-1-1 season 10, it seemed like a chance for significant growth — and it was, but in an unexpected way. Unfortunately, caring for children is complicated, especially when they’ve been orphaned and traumatized. Buck realized he couldn’t care for Theo and gave him up, leading Eddie to foster the kid without telling Buck. To say this caused friction this season would be an understatement.

Buck and Eddie have been at odds for most of the season's first six episodes because they have different parenting styles (and, of course, Buck felt betrayed that Eddie didn’t tell him he was taking Theo in). Watching them dance around each other has been uncomfortable and disappointing for Buddie fans, so it was a relief when they finally reconciled in the 9-1-1 mid-season finale. However, they only kind of reconciled. They might have more issues to work through when 9-1-1 season 10 returns in January.

9-1-1 season 10, episode 6, “Et Tu?” crammed a lot in, including a rather poignant plot for Buck involving a woman he meets at his addiction support meeting. The two click, and Buck temporarily falls back into old bad habits. He can't handle his feelings, and they sleep together. However, between the introduction of the woman’s child and her nearly dying from an overdose, Buck ends up facing his feelings about the Theo situation. He admits he doesn’t believe he was the best thing for Theo at the moment, but he loves and misses him.

That finally forces Buck and Eddie to talk, and they apologize to each other. However, it wasn’t quite a proper reconciliation. They didn’t immediately jump back into their old dynamic (as is their norm), and a lingering awkwardness remained, as Buck turned down an invitation to say hello to Theo because he wasn’t ready. While it’s great that 9-1-1 has finally stopped the two from actively fighting, nothing is truly resolved.

Eddie wasn’t wrong to foster Theo, because it was far less traumatizing than putting the kid into the system, but he was wrong not to tell Buck about it. Buck wasn’t wrong in how he felt, but the way he dealt with those feelings was far from healthy. It’s still unclear, though, how much Buck and Eddie really understand where the other is coming from (which is honestly a bit odd considering their years of friendship). Essentially, the two men have a lot of emotional ground to cover for true reconciliation, but that might not happen any time soon.

Buck and Eddie might not be actively fighting or fully mad at each other now, but they also aren’t back to their old selves. Combined with Buck's reluctance to see Theo, it looks like the duo will spend far less time together outside of work once the show returns. Only seeing each other at work, without truly figuring out their issues, will leave them in a limbo where they're professional but still distant.

This sets them up to push each other away. If they get caught up in life outside of work and that life doesn’t involve each other much, it will be easy for them to ignore the elephant in the room. That dynamic could cause them to drift apart. It’s reasonable to believe that 9-1-1 will swoop in and do what it always does by having them slide back into the way they’ve always been, but that would be a disservice to Buck and Eddie’s relationship.

ABC has confirmed its 2027 midseason premiere dates, confirming when viewers can expect new episodes of Will Trent, Shifting Gears, and many more.

Once Buck figures out his feelings and what role he could play in Theo’s life, he and Eddie need to have an actual conversation about their issues. These guys are in their thirties now; 9-1-1 should allow them to act like the actual adults they are. Melodrama is fine, especially when it’s long been the core of the show, but this season’s Eddie and Buck drama could have been solved eons ago with a few beers and honest communication.

9-1-1 season 10, episode 7, premieres Thursday, January 7, 2027, at 8 PM ET.

Why Chris Pratt Was Recast In Taylor Sheridan's Sicario Meets The Terminal List Action Thriller

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Despite being the original lead of Taylor Sheridan's gritty Sicario meets The Terminal List action thriller, here's why Chris Pratt dropped out. While he initially broke through with comic roles on the likes of Parks and Recreation, Chris Pratt's movie and TV show career has seen him taking a hard right into action in recent years.

From The Tomorrow War to Mercy, it's increasingly common to see Pratt playing soldiers or cops. Pratt's Amazon franchise The Terminal List just scored a season three renewal too, alongside a prequel show. In short, the star would be perfect for a Taylor Sheridan military actioner, and that almost came to pass in 2018 when he signed on for Sheridan's thriller F.A.S.T.

This follows a former soldier recruited by the DEA to take down drug dealers in his local town, and the film was set to be Sheridan's next directorial outing following Wind River. That didn't come to pass, though, and while F.A.S.T. will finally arrive nearly a decade later in 2027, it won't be Pratt scowling on the poster whilst holding an assault rifle.

Sheridan was an actor before switching to screenwriting, after realizing his career had peaked with a supporting role on Sons of Anarchy. In a burst of creativity, Sheridan penned his Neo-Western American Frontier trilogy, consisting of Sicario, Hell or High Water and Wind River. F.A.S.T. was another script Sheridan wrote during this era, and one that Warner Bros. greenlit with a budget in the $75 million range (via Dark Horizons).

Chris Pratt soon signed on, but it soon hit a roadblock due to the surprise success of Sheridan's Yellowstone. Sheridan left the director's chair on F.A.S.T. to focus on the latter's second season, while Gavin O'Connor (The Accountant) stepped in as helmer. Ultimately, that version of the film was shelved, as Warners were concerned about making an R-rated film at that budget level.

With the movie dead (at least for the time being), Pratt moved on to other projects and Sheridan became one of the biggest names on television. After a long period away from the big screen, Sheridan resurrected F.A.S.T. as a producer, with his regular collaborator Ben Richardson (who helmed many episodes of 1883 and 1923) as the new director. The ever-busy Pratt did not return, however, and is likely a little too old to portray the role as written.

For a time, it looked like Sheridan would become the next big director of dark, R-rated thrillers and Neo-Westerns, like Walter Hill. Directing F.A.S.T. in 2019 would have been a level up for Sheridan, being a big-budget actioner with a major star. Had he actually made the film, and it proved a success, it's possible his career would have gone in a different direction.

Instead, Sheridan's Yellowstone universe proved a success, and the ever-prolific writer/producer was soon knocking out more hit shows like Lioness and Tulsa King. While he eventually directed a mid-budget movie called Those Who Wish Me Dead in 2021, his TV schedule has kept him away from the types of cinematic thrillers he made his name with.

Of course, Sheridan had no way of knowing that his decision to step away from F.A.S.T. would have such a profound impact on his career. At the time, it was a way to keep control of a TV series that meant a lot to him, but as a consequence, he became an in-demand showrunner. Who knows, had he chosen F.A.S.T. over Yellowstone, maybe Sheridan would be the one directing the upcoming Call of Duty adaptation instead of simply co-writing it; instead, Peter Berg is taking on that responsibility.

For somebody who had never written a script until his 40s, Sheridan learned fast. Sheridan quickly became known for his quotable dialogue and memorable characters, and F.A.S.T. itself won acclaim when the script did the rounds during the late 2010s. There is a glowing review of the thriller on the screenwriting blog ScriptShadow, which praises how Sheridan takes a familiar premise but approaches it from fresh angles.

The review specifically cites a scene where the main character is talking with a therapist about his wartime trauma. While F.A.S.T.'s protagonist keeps claiming he's fine, throughout his session he is flashing back to horrific combat experiences. It also sounds like the story has a lot in common with Lioness, and deals with the murkier side of warfare as the main character leads a misfit band of soldiers into a private war.

One of the downsides of Sheridan's move to television is that the lean writing style that made his films so great has fallen away. Outside of 1883, some of his shows - especially later seasons of Yellowstone - felt padded simply to fill an episode count. Part of what makes F.A.S.T. so exciting is that it will be a one-and-done action thriller, penned during the same creative run that led to Sicario and Wind River.

It's unknown if Pratt was approached about returning to F.A.S.T., but it's for the best that he moved on. The movie isn't a million miles from The Terminal List, which is also about a former soldier going beyond the law. As written, F.A.S.T.'s lead is a younger character, and the role has now gone to Brandon Sklenar.

Sklenar earned his big breakthrough playing manly hunter Spencer Dutton in 1923, and he has since appeared in hits like The Housemaid with Sydney Sweeney. F.A.S.T. will be his first major test as a leading man, and Sheridan clearly has faith in his 1923 star to pull it off. It helps that the 2027 thriller surrounds Sklenar with a supporting cast that includes screen vets like Jason Clarke, LaKeith Stanfield and Trevante Rhodes (Moonlight).

With F.A.S.T. and Call of Duty, it seems Sheridan is working his way back towards theaters. He hasn't directed a film since 2021, but if F.A.S.T. proves a hit, he might finally step back behind the viewfinder. The movie doesn't have much in the way of competition in May 2027 - though time will tell how audiences greet Mel Gibson's The Resurrection of the Christ: Part One when it arrives earlier that month.

The 8 Grittiest Hard Sci-Fi Movies, Ranked

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

What, exactly, makes a movie "gritty" is a relatively subjective matter. For the most part, though, these are films defined by a harsh, unpolished, and realistic tone. They prioritize raw emotional potency over any sort of elegant escapism, and are often visually desaturated, grounded in their storytelling, and full of flawed characters and sharp social commentary. One genre that gets along particularly well with gritty filmmaking is science fiction, as has been demonstrated by many an incredible masterpiece over the years.

But where "soft" sci-fi (a branch of the genre that focuses on human emotions and soft sciences like sociology and political science) is typically more polished, "hard" sci-fi films (which adhere as strictly as possible to real-world physics and natural laws) are often far better-suited for gritty tones. From underappreciated modern gems like Sunshine to foundational classics like THX 1138, plenty of the greatest hard science fiction films ever made can very reasonably be described as gritty in their stories, tone, or aesthetics.

Danny Boyle has time and again proven himself to be one of the most versatile filmmakers currently working in Hollywood and in the UK, and some of his most unique works come from his collaborations with screenwriter Alex Garland. Case in point: Sunshine, which Garland was inspired to write after learning of scientific ideas regarding the heat death of the universe. Boyle and Garland worked closely with scientific advisers like particle physicist Brian Cox in order to ensure the film was as scientifically plausible as possible.

Whenever the creatives behind a hard sci-fi movie hire scientific advisers, that's how you know that you're in for a hell of a ride—and that is, indeed, the best way to describe Sunshine. But even though the movie pivots softly into the realm of far softer science fiction in its third act, it's still one of the most perfect R-rated hard sci-fi movies ever, with a uniquely gritty atmosphere coming largely from the pure, almost surreal existential dread generated by its premise.

While it's almost inarguably among the most underappreciated sci-fi movies of the 21st century so far, Duncan Jones' Moon (his directorial debut) has nevertheless only continued to grow into more and more of a cult classic as the years have passed. It was made on a modest budget and made an equally modest amount of money at the box office, but with the brilliance of Nathan Parker's script and of Sam Rockwell's lead performance, it may well be one of the best hard sci-fi films of the last 26 years.

Moon is not gritty in that it's action-packed or filled with complicated conflicts, but rather in how visceral its story about corporate exploitation and how corporations see humans as disposable really is. Add to that a grimy, desperately industrial aesthetic and the strong sense of psychological realism of Rockwell's performance, and you get one of the most psychologically intense science fiction thrillers that the big screen has ever seen.

It's not perfect, but Sebastián Cordero's Europa Report is nonetheless one of the most intense hard sci-fi movies ever made, as well as one of the most unique found footage movies of all time. Grounded in scientific realism and made to feel all the more matter-of-fact by its found-footage approach to the story, it's a low-budget masterpiece whose non-linear style truly creates a sense of immersion much akin to watching a documentary about space exploration.

To ensure realistic depictions of gravity, radiation physics, and the surface geography of the eponymous Jupiter moon, the people behind the film actually consulted NASA scientists, and it paid off in spades. But on top of feeling scientifically plausible from beginning to end, Europa Report feels unique thanks to how much of an anti-Hollywood depiction of survival in space it presents. Stripping away any traditional form of sci-fi heroism, the movie instead depicts space as a completely indifferent vacuum that kills indiscriminately and punishes any kind of error. It's a cold, unceremonious movie, and hard sci-fi fans wouldn't have it any other way.

Many of history's greatest science fiction movies—hard sci-fi certainly included—have been substantially divisive, and Claire Denis' High Life is no exception. Starring Robert Pattinson at the top of his game, it's one of the most perfect hard sci-fi movies of the past 10 years, and it was developed with the help of physicist and black hole expert Aurélien Barrau as a consultant. Visually stunning and intellectually challenging in ways that not just everyone might appreciate, it's nevertheless one of the most potently thought-provoking sci-fi films in recent memory.

It's a boldly arthouse-coded approach to the genre, one that subverts traditional science fiction tropes by turning its focus inward to the decaying bodies and psyches of its crew, rather than the cold void of space. This makes it a masterpiece that feels far more psychologically gritty than it does physically or aesthetically gritty, and that works surprisingly well under Denis' magnificent direction. It's a movie full of blood, sweat, tears, and all other sorts of bodily fluids, making it irresistibly yet unceremoniously realistic.

Before he revolutionized not only the science fiction genre, but cinema as a whole, with 1977's Star Wars, George Lucas made his feature directorial debut with the social science fiction masterpiece THX 1138. Based on Lucas' 1967 student film Electronic Labyrinth: THX 1138 4EB, the movie received mixed reviews from critics and underperformed at the box office upon release, but in the years since, it has become one of the biggest hard sci-fi cult classics of the entire 20th century.

THX 1138 crafts a totalitarian dystopian society which feels so real that it's nothing short of terrifying.

It's an alienating, deeply alienating kind of sci-fi movie, with a gritty tone that feels as soul-crushingly visceral as it does terrifyingly sterile. Without any horror elements of any sort, THX 1138 crafts a totalitarian dystopian society which feels so real that it's nothing short of terrifying. Many dystopian movies that are described as "gritty" use rusty metal, rain-slicked urban hellscapes, and literal grit in order to achieve that kind of tone, but not THX. Instead, Lucas achieves that feeling of detachment from blindingly white and overwhelmingly empty sterility.

With its having made nearly $800 million dollars at the box office worldwide, Gravity is Alfonso Cuarón's second-highest-grossing movie to date by a pretty decent margin. It's science fiction that leans far more toward the "science" part than the "fiction" part, and that's exactly what makes it one of the greatest hard science fiction movies that the world has seen in years. Led by Sandra Bullock at her very best, it's a must-see for fans of the genre.

There's a wide variety of reasons why Gravity is one of the most masterfully gritty films of its kind, not least of which is the fact that it's among the most perfectly-written hard sci-fi movies ever made. Add to that some of the most groundbreaking CGI that the film industry has ever seen, and it should be perfectly understandable how Cuarón managed to make a film that's so exhausting, so powerfully claustrophobic, and so sensorially punishing.

Shane Carruth's Primer is a cult classic that's absolutely legendary among fans of sci-fi movies made on shoestring budgets, and for good reason. Made for less than $10,000 dollars, it's nevertheless one of the best—and most fascinatingly realistic—time travel movies ever made. Carruth studied physics in order to help him make the plot plausible and the characters' technical dialogue sound as authentic as possible, and the research definitely paid off.

Indeed, Primer is one of those hard sci-fi movies that are perfect from the first scene to the last, a love letter to science fiction filmmaking that's as dedicated as possible to scientific and technological realism. That commitment to realism inevitably ends up translating in the form of pure grittiness as well, which is further boosted by the visceral aesthetics of the movie, taking full advantage of its admirably low budget.

Right after he established himself as a true Hollywood titan with 2004's Harry Potter and the Prisoner of Azkaban, Alfonso Cuarón was given a creative blank check to adapt P. D. James' 1992 novel The Children of Men. The result? 2006's Children of Men, which bombed at the box office at the time of its release, but soon started gaining more and more of a cult following. Today, it's so universally acclaimed that it's just about as mainstream of a modern sci-fi classic as they come.

Calling Children of Men the single grittiest hard sci-fi movie of all time is a take as cold as Antarctica. It's grit that you can practically feel deep in your body, present across virtually all aspects of the movie: Its dark story, its dystopian world-building, its damp and dirty visuals, and its gray cinematography. From top to bottom and from beginning to end, Children of Men serves as a masterclass in how to make an apocalyptic sci-fi film that, while undeniably gritty, also has a refreshingly hopeful and humanist core.

Where to find the Fast & Furious Mitsubishi Eclipse in GTA 6

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

How to get to Vespucci Island and get yourself a 10-second car

How to get to Vespucci Island and get yourself a 10-second car

In GTA 6, Brian O'Conner's green Mitsubishi Eclipse from the first Fast & Furious movie can be yours if you know where to look. The car is a direct reference to the one we see Paul Walker's character driving early in the movie. It's a pretty fast car that can help you to quickly traverse the map and escape the police. Considering how good this car is, it's surprisingly easy to rob, making it a perfect special car to look for early in the game — as long as you have the funds to keep it for yourself.

Below, you learn where to find Brian's green Mitsubishi Eclipse in GTA 6.

To get the chance to add Brian's Mitsubishi Eclipse, also known as the '97 Maibatsu Penumbra FF, to your collection, you must go to Vespucci Island in Vice-Dale County. This area is on the east coast, and Vespucci Island is one of three Rialto Islands in the Vice Beach region, northeast of Vice City.

Once you reach Vespucci Island, turn left onto the street heading north when coming from the west. Right on the corner, opposite a construction site, you will see a big house with a white gate. As you approach the place, the gate will automatically open. There are no guards around, so don't worry about NPCs spotting you entering the house.

Go to the door beside the garage. It's locked, and your only option is to break it, but you must be quick, because as soon as you break the door, the alarm will go off. Inside the garage, you will find the green Mitsubishi Eclipse. Open the garage then get into the car. If you have already met Moses in Chapter 2, you can use the slim jim to open the car without smashing the window.

After you get out of the house, you can pay $6,750 to register it. You can also sell it for $1,100, but this car is excellent and you should consider keeping it. Here's its stats:

If you're just getting started in GTA 6, we have plenty of guides to help you live the high life in Leonida. Check out our GTA 6 beginner guide to get a grasp of the basics and our tips for what to do first, recommendations for the best ways to make money fast, and an explainer for how to keep stolen weapons and cars to make the most of your free time.

Our selection of GTA 6 guides includes help for things like how to steal the Phoenix in "Unwanted Attention" and whether you should defuse Mandy, too. There's a set of explainers for GTA 6's more open-ended side activities, like how to rob the Exotic Animal Front and where to find tree effigies for the Skunk Ape mission, and we've got all the GTA 6 cheat codes. You'll have to put in some effort before you can use those, though.

HBO's 9-Hour Bingeable Masterpiece Is White Lotus Meets Severance

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

HBO's 18-episode tragicomedy only takes 18 hours to watch, and it's White Lotus meets Severance. We are missing 30-minute TV shows. It seems like every series now has to have 50-65 minute episodes, or it's not going to get made. 30 minutes is plenty of time to get whatever story you need to tell, told.

Enlightened understands this. HBO's 18-episode series from Mike White is a quick binge, but there is so much humor and drama packed into it that you'll feel like you've been watching the show for much longer. White wrote the series with the star Laura Dern, who plays Amy Jellicoe, a 40-year-old woman who has a public breakdown at work.

Once a high-powered VP, Amy takes a sabbatical and returns to work at her old company, Abaddonn, with a new outlook that does not convince her old coworkers, lovers, and family that she's actually changed. Now at the bottom rung of a company where she used to have a seat at the top, Amy has to start over in more ways than one.

The brightly-lit basement of Abaddonn, filled with odd, quirky, unambitious employees, feels just like the sterile offices of Severance, and Amy's forced smiles at work feel like the out-of-body personalities of the people in the Apple TV hit. However, Mike White's empathetic, though acid-laced writing makes for a more moving, though more harshly relatable story that fans of The White Lotus will recognize.

Enlightened is the third TV show that White created, after Pasadena in 2001 and Cracking Up in 2004. Enlightened is the first series where he has a writing credit on every episode and multiple directing credits, and it's clear this is where the White-style of filmmaking is getting honed.

Most people will have seen The White Lotus before Enlightened, and when returning to this series, you'll immediately see the similarities between the two shows. The filmmaking is strikingly similar. There are the same lens flares that The White Lotus is known for, and the panning camera shots showing people moving through gorgeous sets and vistas.

There's a similar sense of humor, with people's foibles and failures providing tough but funny jokes. White's empathy shines through as well. There are a lot of unlikable people in Enlightened, just as there are in The White Lotus, but White clearly feels for these people, and his dialogue and story encourage you to pity the worst of them.

Unfortunately, Enlightened never got the ending it deserved. Despite rave reviews from critics, including an 87% on Rotten Tomatoes and a Golden Globe win for Dern, along with a Golden Globe nomination for Best Musical or Comedy, Enlightened was canceled after its second season.

The cancelation came right after the season 2 finale, and sadly the series comes to a sudden, unfulfilling stop. Perhaps this is why White has made The White Lotus an anthology that can absorb a sudden cancelation. Despite its unfinished nature, Enlightened is absolutely worth watching; you'll just be left wanting more.

We are getting the next best thing to Enlightened season 3, though. It has been announced that Dern will be reuniting with White in The White Lotus season 4. She will replace Helena Bonham Carter, who left the project in early 2026. Dern had a voice cameo in The White Lotus season two as Abby, Dominic's (Michael Imperioli) estranged wife, and it's unclear if she will be reprising that role or playing someone entirely new.

DC’s ‘Supergirl’ Officially Falls Behind New 3-Hour Mythological Fantasy Blockbuster

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

He has covered everything from Marvel to the Oscars, and Marvel at the Oscars. He also writes obsessively about the box office, charting the many hits and misses that are released weekly, and how their commercial performance shapes public perception. In his time at Collider, he has also helped drive diversity by writing stories about the multiple Indian film industries, with a goal of introducing audiences to a whole new world of cinema.

For the third weekend in a row, two international titles have secured spots on the top 10 list at the domestic box office, although the gap between them is increasing. The two movies — Japan's Godzilla Minus Zero and India's Ramayana: Rise of a Legend — opened theatrically on November 6. Godzilla Minus Zero claimed the number-one spot on the weekend leaderboard, but saw a precipitous drop in revenue in its second week. The movie had another hefty drop this weekend, as it clung to the fifth spot. Ramayana, on the other hand, has held better over the weeks, even if its overall revenue is roughly half of Godzilla Minus Zero's.

The Indian epic is based on a mythological text sacred to Hindus all over the world. Stories in the text form the basis of Hindu festivals such as Diwali and Dussehra, representing the victory of good over evil. In the text, the virtuous Lord Rama goes on a mission to vanquish the evil Ravana after the demon king of Lanka kidnaps the Lord's wife, Sita. Ramayana is the first installment of a planned duology that cost more than $400 million to produce, according to its producer.

This makes Ramayana the most expensive Indian film project in history, with the first installment's presumed $200 million budget was roughly triple the previous record for an Indian film, set by​​​​​​​ Kalki 2898 AD. The upcoming action-adventure movie Varanasi, directed by RRR filmmaker S.S. Rajamouli, reportedly carries a $150 million budget and will be released in 2027. By then, Ramayana will have cemented itself as the highest-grossing Indian film at the North American box office.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

The new movie has grossed roughly $28 million so far, having overtaken this year's Dhurandhar: The Revenge to become the top-grossing Indian film ever in North America. It has, of course, generated the majority of its revenue in its home country. Ramayana's global box-office haul now stands at around $170 million — it still hasn't broken even at the box office, although the economics of Indian films aren't the same as those of their American counterparts.

As things stand, Ramayana has not only overtaken the lifetime global hauls of RRR and Dhurandhar 2, but also Hollywood movies such as Supergirl, The Sheep Detectives, and most recently, Insidious: Out of the Further. Directed by Nitesh Tiwari and starring Ranbir Kapoor and Yash, the film holds a 77% critics' score and a "Verified Hot" 93% audience score on the aggregator website Rotten Tomatoes. Stay tuned to Collider for more updates.

10 HBO Crime Shows That Are Perfect Weekend Binges

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

Crime shows have been a TV staple for decades, and the reason is fairly simple. The truth is that everyone loves a good mystery. The problem, however, is that the genre is so crowded that most crime shows have started to blur together. Too many of them lean on the same familiar formulas featuring brooding detectives with a dark past and last-minute twists that only exist for shock value. This means very few have the potential to leave a lasting impression.

HBO has always taken a different approach, though. The network uses the genre as a vehicle to explore broken families, corrupt institutions, and personal demons. In doing that, these series are never afraid to leave viewers a little uncomfortable, instead of offering them perfect closure. Speaking of which, many of them also tell a complete story in a season or two. Here is a list of short HBO crime series that make for a perfect weekend binge.

The Night Of shows just how quickly one bad decision can unravel someone's entire life. Nasir “Naz” Khan (Riz Ahmed) is a quiet Pakistani-American college student from Queens who takes his father’s taxi without asking so he can go to a party in Manhattan. Instead, he ends up spending the night with a young woman named Andrea, and when he wakes up, she has been stabbed to death. Naz is arrested within hours, and with no memory of what happened, his only hope is John Stone (John Turturro), a low-rent lawyer who usually handles petty cases.

What sets The Night Of apart from other crime dramas is how closely it follows the legal system itself. The show walks the audience through every step of Naz’s case, from the police station to Rikers Island and finally the courtroom, and it becomes painfully clear that nobody involved is really interested in the truth. Everyone on both sides is more focused on winning the case than on what actually happened that night. By the end, the question isn’t just whether Naz is guilty, but what the system has turned him into. The Night Of is slow and patient, and it remains one of the most gripping crime dramas HBO has ever made because it asks the hard questions instead of delivering a typical whodunit.

Mare of Easttown feels less like a crime show and more like an accurate portrayal of how toxic and suffocating small-town life can be. The series features Kate Winslet as Mare Sheehan, a Pennsylvania detective who is still grieving her son’s death while fighting to keep custody of her grandson. To make things worse, she is also haunted by a missing persons case she never managed to solve, which has led to the entire town growing increasingly skeptical of her. When teenage mother Erin McMenamin (Cailee Spaeny) is found dead in the woods, Mare is put in charge of the investigation, and every lead points back to people she grew up with.

Most crime dramas rush to the next clue, but Mare of Easttown takes its time to show what the job costs the person doing it. Mare can’t separate her work from her life, because in a town this small, every suspect is someone she has a connection to. The mystery is full of clever twists, but the show is really about grief and how people carry on after the worst has happened. It’s also surprisingly funny at times, especially in Mare’s prickly scenes with her mother, Helen (Jean Smart). Mare of Easttown is a tight mystery with real heart, and its final reveal is one of the most devastating in recent TV, and the fact that it manages to do all that in just seven episodes makes it all the more brilliant.

Big Little Lies is surprisingly gripping, even if it is a story about a bunch of rich, suburban moms. The series is set in the wealthy coastal town of Monterey, California, and opens with news of a death at a school fundraiser without revealing who died or who is responsible. From there, the story rewinds to follow this group of mothers whose lives become tangled together. Madeline (Reese Witherspoon) is outspoken and loves a good feud, while Celeste (Nicole Kidman) appears to have the perfect marriage. They soon befriend Jane (Shailene Woodley), a young single mom who has just moved to town with her son, and that’s when things start to take a turn.

The genius of Big Little Lies is how it hides a heavy story inside what looks like a glossy soap opera. At first, it’s easy to get caught up in the playground drama and petty rivalries between the moms. Then, the show peels back the surface to reveal the trauma and abuse some of these women are living with behind closed doors. The mystery of the fundraiser death keeps the story moving, but what keeps the viewer coming back is the messy, complex friendship that forms between these women who start as rivals. Big Little Lies is still airing, with Season 3 in production, but its first two installments work perfectly on their own for an addictive weekend binge.

Perry Mason tells the story of TV’s most famous lawyer and gets right into his gritty origin story. The series, set in 1930s Los Angeles, introduces Perry Mason (Matthew Rhys) as a broke, burned-out private investigator who is still haunted by his time in World War I. His life takes a turn when he is hired to dig into the kidnapping and murder of a baby, a case that quickly grabs the entire city’s attention. As the investigation pulls him into a web of corrupt cops and powerful church leaders, Perry starts down the path that will eventually lead him to the courtroom.

Fans of the classic Raymond Burr series might not recognize this version of Perry at first, and that’s the point. The show strips away the lawyer’s polished image and replaces it with a flawed man who has to earn his place as a lawyer, often by bending the rules along the way. The show’s version of 1930s Los Angeles is gorgeous, but the city feels rotten because here, money and power decide who is guilty. The courtroom scenes in Perry Mason are a masterclass in suspenseful storytelling, and they make the lawyer’s hard-won victories feel genuinely earned. Perry Mason ended after two seasons, but it’s a stylish, old-school crime drama that never stops feeling exhilarating.

Tokyo Vice offers both style and substance by giving the audience a rare look at Japanese organized crime through the eyes of an outsider. The series is based on Jake Adelstein’s memoir and follows Ansel Elgort as the crime reporter who lands a job at a major Tokyo newspaper in 1999. Now, Jake is eager to prove himself and starts digging into stories his editors would rather he leave alone. Naturally, it doesn’t take long before he’s tangled up with the yakuza. Along the way, he teams up with veteran detective Hiroto Katagiri (Ken Watanabe), who is fighting his own battle against the city’s criminal underworld.

Tokyo Vice treats Tokyo as more than just a backdrop. The neon-lit streets and smoky hostess clubs feel alive, and the show takes its time explaining how the city’s unwritten rules shape its citizens’ choices. Jake’s fish-out-of-water story keeps things accessible, but the show’s best moments often belong to the yakuza themselves, who are written as complicated people rather than one-dimensional villains. The series is slower than a typical crime thriller, and with two seasons, it’s a longer binge. Still, Tokyo Vice is one of the most immersive crime shows on HBO Max.

Four criminal masterminds. One of them was always going to be yours. Here is who comes for you, why they picked you out of everyone available, and what they want from the encounter.

Your nemesis does not want to beat you. He wants to know you completely, and he is patient enough to wait for it. The frightening part is how good the conversation is.

Your nemesis is not after money, territory or revenge. He is after a demonstration, and you are the person he has chosen to prove his point on.

Your nemesis respects you, which sounds flattering until you work out what it means. He chose you because nobody else was worth his time.

Your nemesis has no personal interest in you whatsoever, and that is precisely what makes him unsurvivable. You are not a rival. You are a stop on the way.

The Penguin proves that a great crime story doesn’t necessarily need a superhero. The show picks up right after the events of The Batman and follows low-level gangster Oz Cobb (Colin Farrell), who sees his chance to climb to the top of Gotham’s underworld after the death of crime boss Carmine Falcone. His biggest obstacle is Carmine’s daughter, Sofia (Cristin Milioti), who has just been released from Arkham Asylum and wants revenge on the people who put her there. Along the way, Oz takes a young man named Victor Aguilar (Rhenzy Feliz) under his wing and drags him into a war he isn’t ready for.

The Penguin plays out much more like a classic mob drama than a typical comic book show, and that’s exactly why it works so well. Farrell portrays Oz as a hungry, resentful man who has spent his whole life being underestimated. That backstory makes him both sympathetic and terrifying. The show pairs his rise with Sofia’s story, and the clash between two outcasts fighting for the same throne is easily the most gripping part of the story. Victor’s relationship with Oz adds another layer of complexity to the drama, and it’s hard to watch him slowly fall under Oz’s influence. Viewers don’t need to know a thing about Batman to get hooked, and by the finale, The Penguin stands shoulder to shoulder with HBO’s greatest mob dramas.

The Undoing is a glamorous Manhattan thriller about how little people really know about the ones they love. The story follows Grace Fraser (Nicole Kidman), a successful therapist married to charming pediatric oncologist Jonathan (Hugh Grant), and their life together seems perfect. However, everything falls apart when a young mother from their son’s private school is found brutally murdered, and Jonathan suddenly disappears. As the police close in, Grace is forced to question everything she thought she knew about her husband.

The Undoing is the kind of show that makes viewers change their minds about the killer every single episode. The audience experiences the entire ordeal through Grace’s eyes, so every new piece of evidence feels like a personal betrayal. Grant plays Jonathan with an ambiguity that makes it impossible for one to tell whether he really is a devoted husband or a serial killer who is great at covering his tracks. The murder essentially bursts the privileged bubble the Frasers live in, and that is one of the most important parts of the narrative. Every episode delivers a twist that turns the entire story on its head and makes it impossible for the viewers to stop watching until they have all the answers.

The Jinx is the only documentary on this list, but it’s every bit as gripping as any scripted show. The series, directed by Andrew Jarecki, follows Robert Durst, the eccentric heir to a New York real estate empire who has been linked to several deaths over the decades. To complicate things further, his wife, Kathie, vanished in 1982 and was never found. Not just that, but years later, both his close friend Susan Berman and a neighbor in Texas turned up dead. Surprisingly, Durst agrees to sit down with Jarecki for hours of interviews, and what he says on camera changes the course of the case.

The Jinx is fascinating because Durst himself is the most compelling thing about it. He is both awkward and strangely charismatic in his interviews, and it’s impossible to tell what he is lying about as he tries to explain himself. The series builds its case slowly, and by the final episode, the tension is almost unbearable. The Jinx’s ending is one of the most shocking moments in documentary history, one that actually had real-world consequences. The Jinx Season aired in 2024 to follow what happened next, and together, the two installments make the docuseries essential viewing for any true crime fan.

We Own This City comes from The Wire creator David Simon, so it had pretty big shoes to fill. Thankfully, the series stands on its own as a look into the true story of the Baltimore Police Department’s Gun Trace Task Force, an elite unit that was meant to take illegal guns off the streets. However, under Sgt. Wayne Jenkins (Jon Bernthal), the officers robbed the very people they were supposed to protect and lied their way into huge overtime checks. When civil rights investigator Nicole Steele (Wunmi Mosaku) is brought in to look into the department, she realizes the problem runs much deeper than one rogue unit.

What’s interesting is that We Own This City never treats Jenkins as the whole problem. He gets away with his crimes for years because he brings in the arrests and seized guns his bosses want to see, and nobody bothers to ask how he managed to do it. The show also spends time with the people who pay the price for that corruption, without ever feeling preachy. The story jumps between timelines, so it takes some focus, but the payoff is well worth it. By the end, We Own This City leaves the viewers angry but informed, and that is exactly what a show like this can aim to achieve.

The Staircase revisits one of the most debated true crime cases of the 21st century, and it somehow makes it even more fascinating. In 2001, crime novelist Michael Peterson (Colin Firth) calls 911 to report that his wife, Kathleen (Toni Collette), has fallen down the stairs of their North Carolina home. The police aren’t convinced it was an accident, and Michael is soon charged with her murder. The trial tears his large blended family apart, while a French documentary crew captures every moment of the case on camera.

The Staircase absolutely refuses to tell the audience what to think, but most importantly, it turns a courtroom story into a family drama. Michael’s children grew up loving and trusting him, and the show captures how painful it is for them to see their father in a brand-new light, not knowing whether they can trust him any longer. Michael himself is a writer who knows how to tell a good story, and the show cleverly leaves the audience asking whether he’s doing the same thing with his own life. The Staircase is less about the fall itself and more about everything that came after, and it’s the perfect show to close out a weekend.

Life in Isolation: Are We Living in a Human Zoo?

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 22, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

In Stolen Focus, Johann Hari explores the distress of captive animals through veterinary behaviorist Nicholas Dodman's description of "frustrated biological objectives": innate needs that remain active even when circumstances prevent their expression. Zoochosis describes abnormal behaviors observed in captive animals, including compulsive self-directed activity.

Let’s consider if zoochosis could apply to human beings too: could some of our depression and anxiety be less about defective individuals and more about the increasingly unnatural environments we live in? We recognize something is wrong when a tiger paces endlessly in a concrete enclosure. Yet when human beings experience restlessness, alienation, depression, or anxiety, we are remarkably quick to diagnose them. “I don’t know who discovered water but I doubt it was a fish,” said Marshall McLuhan. Maybe it’s the water that surrounds us that’s toxic and not something inside of us?

Remote work, online shopping, food delivery, streaming entertainment, and virtual socializing have become increasingly normal since the pandemic. Many of these things are genuinely convenient and some have improved people's quality of life. But maybe our frictionless lives are not necessarily an upgrade to the human experience? Maybe we thrive when we have little victories like trips to the store, restaurants, gyms and other third spaces where we interact with others?

We work on one screen, order necessities through another, socialize through messages, exercise in front of a monitor, and unwind by scrolling through videos of other people living lives that appear to be more interesting and exciting than our own. We can spend days on end interacting with two-dimensional representations of the world without actually being face-to-face with other people. Ever heard of pheromones?

We accumulate hundreds of online acquaintances without experiencing the vulnerability and authenticity that we need and crave. We communicate constantly while remaining profoundly lonely.

Humans evolved to move through complex physical terrains, not sit on couches with laptops on our laps. We need touching, exploring, smelling, creating, cooperating, taking risks, and belonging to communities in three dimensions, not two.

And being constantly available is not our friend. Neither is excessive optimization nor compulsive productivity. Having no time for friends, family, or contemplation should be seen as failure. We can no longer simply take a walk; we need to track our steps, monitor our heart rates, and demonstrate that we have accomplished something in contrast to “just walking.”

So what if some of what we call individual psychopathology is really a response to collective conditions that frustrate fundamental human needs?

Psychology should examine not only what patients are thinking and feeling but also take into account the influences that are causing people to think and feel the way they do. We should ask questions such as, “Do they have true agency? Are they physically engaged with the world? Do they experience meaningful relationships, belonging, creativity, and contribution? Do they have opportunities to experience spontaneity, wonder, and delight?”

Perhaps we need to reconsider the conditions under which we expect human beings to live, work, love, and flourish. That means examining not only individual choices but also workplaces, economic incentives, social institutions, and the technologies that increasingly mediate our experience of reality.

Helping people adapt to their circumstances is an important part of psychotherapy but our adaptation to COVID-lockdown reclusiveness may be more akin to zoochosis than we have hitherto believed.

Hari, J. (2022). "Stolen Focus: Why You Can't Pay Attention—and How to Think Deeply Again." Crown.

The Week 11 afternoon schedule is short on must-see games

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

As the NFL’s haves and the have-nots continue to declare themselves, it’s becoming challenging at times to find compelling games.

And while the coming week has five of them in national, standalone windows (Vikings-49ers, Packers-Rams, Bears-Lions, Chiefs-Bills, and Broncos-Steelers), the eleventh Sunday is a bit lacking. To say the least.

In the early window, three of the seven games feature double-digit spreads. The 8-1 Bills (-16.5) host the 1-8 Dolphins, who don’t have their starting quarterback. The 7-2 Chiefs (-15.5) host the 3-7 Cardinals, who are down to their fourth quarterback of the season — and the third in three weeks.

The 7-2 Bears (-11.5) host the 3-6 Saints.

None of the early games feature two teams with winning records. Bucs-Lions is arguably the most compelling of the window, given the potential for Detroit stepping into a trap four days before the Thanksgiving showdown against the Bears.

The late time slot is led by the 7-2 Jaguars (-6) at the 5-3-1 Giants. It’s the only game of the weekend featuring a pair of division leaders.

Having six teams on a bye this weekend doesn’t help.

Although it’s impossible in May to know how the various games of a given schedule will look in late November (Steelers-Eagles had the potential on paper to be the best game of the day), it’s a convenient time for a subpar Sunday. With millions still scratching their GTA itch, they can fully focus on Vice City this afternoon before the Vikings and 49ers square off tonight in Mexico City.

GTA 6 Could Actually Lose at Least 3 Game Awards Categories

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

This may not be a Clair Obscur-style sweep

After racking up an astonishing 99 rating on Metacritic — officially the highest ever — Grand Theft Auto 6 is more than the favorite to win Game of the Year at The Game Awards on Dec. 10. It's a certainty.

But this doesn't necessarily mean we're looking at an awards-night sweep of the kind Clair Obscur: Expedition 33 pulled off at the 2025 awards. There are one or two categories where GTA 6 may well lose, and it will need to win in every single one to match Clair Obscur's record nine wins. (GTA 6 has eight nominations, but that will surely go up to nine when the nominees for the fan-voted Players' Voice award are announced.)

Let's break it down, category by category:

Locked in. There's no way GTA 6 is not winning GOTY.

This is also very likely a win for GTA 6. It's rare for Best Game Direction not to go to the eventual GOTY winner. When it does happen, it's usually in years when there's no clear front-runner. That can be down to a weak field, as in 2021, when It Takes Two won Game of the Year and Deathloop won Best Direction, or an intensely competitive one like 2023, when Baldur's Gate 3 took GOTY and Alan Wake 2 won Game Direction. The gulf between GTA 6 and the rest of the field is so wide, I can't see another game winning Game Direction this time, even considering perennial awards darling (and two-time Game Direction winner) Remedy Entertainment has Control Resonant up in the same category this year.

Again, I think GTA 6 is ahead here, although it has strong competition from The Blood of Dawnwalker in particular. But Rockstar won Best Narrative for Red Dead Redemption 2 in 2018, and GTA 6 definitely shares that game's more mature storytelling style, while the majority of reviews highlighted how strongly the love story between Jason and Lucia anchors the game.

GTA 6 is an amazing-looking game, both technically and artistically, with acutely observed character looks and astounding environmental design. But The Game Awards tends not to reward outright realism in a contemporary setting in the Art Direction category. For example, in 2020, The Last of Us Part 2 was defeated by the more lush and painterly Ghost of Tsushima in this race.

Remedy is a two-time winner in this category, for Control and Alan Wake 2; the jury loves its combination of AAA sheen and striking, surreal visual design. It's the favorite, but I could see a win for The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time here too — as a dramatic and very successful reinterpretation of a classic, Nintendo's game certainly has the most noticeable art direction of the year.

Will win: The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time

This is a tight race. GTA 6's soundtrack defies belief, but much of that rests on its vast library of licensed music, and there will be a perception (rightly or wrongly) that Rockstar flexed its massive buying power here rather than exercising thoughtful curation. More to the point, the Game Awards jury does not tend to reward licensed soundtracks in this category, preferring traditional, movie-style musical scores. (If there's a Final Fantasy game nominated, it's winning.)

So I think this award will rest mostly on Justice's score, and while the French electronic duo is a cool get for Rockstar, I'm not sure their moody synthwave can stand up to the timeless melodies of Koji Kondo in Nintendo's stunning new orchestrations. (Watch out for Ace Combat 8 sneaking up on the outside of this race, too.)

In the understanding of The Game Awards jury, more audio is better audio, so I think GTA 6's teeming soundscape will win the day.

Will win: Manni L. Perez as Lucia Caminos in GTA 6

Angela Sant'Albano has been the favorite all year for her turn as Resident Evil: Requiem's Grace, but Manni Perez is making a huge splash as Lucia. It's not quite a lock. This is one category where recency bias doesn't necessarily work in your favor; it's quite common for the Best Performance winner to feature in a game released earlier in the year, with a character players have spent more time with. Still, Lucia is undeniable.

In recent years, this category has reflected the opinions of Chinese gamers, who overwhelm the vote with a patriotic pick. Three of the last four Players' Voice winners have been Chinese games: Genshin Impact, Black Myth: Wukong, and Wuthering Waves. Phantom Blade Zero is arguably stronger than any of them.

I've got GTA 6 winning six of nine categories, which would put it joint third among all-time Game Awards winners, alongside Baldur's Gate 3 and God of War Ragnarӧk. (The Last of Us Part 2 won seven awards.) Not too shabby. It would be a good result for The Game Awards, too; as much as I love Clair Obscur, there's nothing more boring than a predictable sweep.

Ridley Scott’s New Blade Runner Series Officially Hits Prime Video This Week

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

He has covered everything from Marvel to the Oscars, and Marvel at the Oscars. He also writes obsessively about the box office, charting the many hits and misses that are released weekly, and how their commercial performance shapes public perception. In his time at Collider, he has also helped drive diversity by writing stories about the multiple Indian film industries, with a goal of introducing audiences to a whole new world of cinema.

After having revived the Lord of the Rings franchise for the small screen with the epic The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power, Prime Video will likely attempt a similar expansion for the James Bond series. Now that Robert Pattinson has been locked as Agent 007 after an intense, months-long casting process, Amazon will get to work on the big-screen reboot in earnest. It wouldn't be surprising if the franchise has a spin-off or two in its future. Before that, however, another iconic brand will make its streaming debut on Prime Video as early as this week.

The upcoming project is a limited series inspired by one of the most influential sci-fi properties of all time: Blade Runner. Unlike the Alien series, which has thrived in various formats since its debut in the 1970s, Ridley Scott's Blade Runner series has struggled to break out into the mainstream. It makes sense that Hollywood is trying to bring the franchise to a broader audience despite the underperformance of a major legacy sequel some years ago.

The original Blade Runner infamously underperformed at the box office as well, grossing $40 million against a reported budget of $30 million in the early 1980s. This was when Harrison Ford was at the peak of his stardom, thanks to the Star Wars and the Indiana Jones franchises. Blade Runner 2049, directed by Denis Villeneuve, couldn't break the curse despite widespread critical acclaim.

Prime Video's latest reboot is the limited series Blade Runner 2099. Starring Michelle Yeoh and Hunter Schafer in the lead roles, the series was created by Silka Luisa, with Scott serving as an executive producer. The filmmaker has played a similar role on the recent Alien: Romulus reboot and the FX series Alien: Earth. He now intends to return to the Alien franchise as a director, picking up the narrative from where he left it in Alien: Covenant.

Incidentally, Covenant was released in the same year as Blade Runner 2049. It grossed $240 million worldwide, compared to Blade Runner 2049's $280 million global haul. Villeneuve's film was vastly better received, although it reportedly cost tens of millions more to produce. Ford has had a tough time working on legacy sequels — besides Blade Runner 2049, he also starred in the massive box-office underperformer Indiana Jones and the Dial of Destiny, and his character, Han Solo, was killed off after appearing in Star Wars: Episode VII — The Force Awakens. You can watch the new Blade Runner 2099 series on Prime Video from November 25, and stay tuned to Collider for more updates.

Kieran Culkin Says a ‘Succession’ Spinoff ‘Would Be a Terrible Idea’: ‘What Are You Hoping For? “Cousin Greg: The Sitcom?”‘

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Sorry, “Succession” fans — Kieran Culkin is shutting down any possibility of a spinoff once and for all.

The actor, currently starring in “The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping” as a young Caesar Flickerman, joined the “Heart Breakfast” radio show on Friday to discuss the new film, though hosts Amanda Holden and Jamie Theakston also snuck in a few questions regarding the hit HBO show’s potential future. “I know we’re not here to talk about this, but I’m still grieving the end of ‘Succession,’” said Holden, to which Culkin responded: “Oh, you are? Get over it. It’s been three years!” When asked about a potential for a spinoff, Culkin answered, “Of course not. I think that would be a terrible idea… I mean, what are you hoping for? ‘Cousin Greg: The Sitcom?’… Yeah, that would be a terrible idea.”

And though the “A Real Pain” actor is steadfast about not retreading the show’s familiar territory, he noted that he misses playing Roman Roy and the “whole thing.”

Culkin continued: “It was fun. It was one of those where Jesse Armstrong, the showrunner, spent the entire last season trying to figure out if there was a way to do one more. I never wanted to know what was going to happen in the season and on the fourth one I did ask and when he pitched me the end of the season and I went ‘well, that kind of sounds like it’s the end of the show.’ But when he pitched me three different ideas of what season 5 could be, they all sounded good… But it ended perfectly. The way it was supposed to.

Star Trek Reboot Officially Teased as Crimson Tide Meets Training Day Sci-Fi Thriller

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Two Denzel Washington classics are strong influences on the new Trek movie

After more than a decade of waiting, it seems we may finally get a new theatrically released Star Trek movie. While dedicated Trekkies have heard that before, the directors of the only known Star Trek movie in development recently shared details about their project. The pair helming the new Trek film, Jonathan Goldstein and John Francis Daley, broke down the movie's influences in an interview with Inverse.

Specifically, they cited Training Day and Crimson Tide as jumping-off points for what they’re going for.

For those unfamiliar with Training Day, it stars Ethan Hawke as a newcomer LAPD narcotics officer under the tutelage of the corrupt detective Alonzo Harris, played by Denzel Washington. As for Crimson Tide, it’s a thriller about a U.S. nuclear submarine facing off against a Russian ultranationalist faction threatening to launch nukes at the U.S. and Japan (interestingly enough, it also stars Washington).

As for how the Training Day influence will show up in the film, Daley said the movie will center on some of the “bad apples” in Starfleet. “I would say it’s really kind of a look at the institution of Starfleet and any institution as a whole that has existed for a long time and what that does,” Daley said to Inverse. “I think the portrayal of Starfleet and the people that work within it have always been generally idealized versions of what the future could be. And while this [script] isn’t a cynical or nihilistic take on it, I think it does acknowledge that there are some bad apples in every institution that can have a strong voice and can really make an impact on that institution and everything it stands for.”

Past Star Trek movies have dealt with Starfleet bad apples before to varying levels of success, including Star Trek VI: The Undiscovered Country, Star Trek: Insurrection, and Star Trek Into Darkness. But Daly and Goldstein have another twist on the Star Trek formula.

“It takes place almost in real time, and it just is a whole other look at what a Star Trek movie could be without betraying the fundamentals of what it should be,” Daley said.

The pair added that the film will center on all-new Star Trek characters. “I mean, look, we don’t want to rely on fandom,” Goldstein said. “We don’t want to just hope that there’s enough fans of the original [TOS] or The Next Generation or whatever who will turn out for this. We want to just make a great movie that lives in the world that people are familiar with — some people are familiar with — and that others will discover. And then, with good fortune, we launch a new franchise into existence.”

Although the film is still only in development, there are reasons to be excited based on Daley and Goldstein’s recent work. The pair previously co-directed and co-wrote the surprisingly fun Dungeons & Dragons: Honor Among Thieves, which starred former James T. Kirk actor Chris Pine. Before that, they co-directed the action comedy Game Night, and their most recent theatrical release, Mayday, also received positive reviews.

Without Daley and Goldstein’s film, Star Trek would unfortunately be at risk of going on ice for a bit. Besides Star Trek: Strange New Worlds and Starfleet Academy, both of which will end after their next seasons, there aren’t any announced TV series in the works. Hopefully, this movie can get the franchise back in Paramount’s good graces.

To boldly go where the series has never gone before

Miley Cyrus Marries Maxx Morando After 5 Years of Dating

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Miley Cyrus and Maxx Morando tied the knot in Palm Springs, Calif., on Nov. 21 after five years together.

Miley Cyrus and Maxx Morando are having the ultimate party in U.S.A.

Nearly one year after the Grammy winner and Liily drummer revealed their engagement, the couple tied the knot on Nov. 21 in Palm Springs, Calif., following five years of dating, according to Entertainment Tonight.

Miley—who was previously married to Liam Hemsworth from 2018 to 2020—began dating Maxx in 2021 after they were set up on a blind date through his bandmate's sister's friend, he told Wonderland magazine in July.

As for the proposal, the couple proved that true love hit them like a wrecking ball when Maxx popped the question during a vacation in Japan, getting down on one knee in a beautiful Japanese garden.

“It was a little dead end, this beautiful Japanese forest-y situation,” Maxx told Wonderland. “She was taking videos, and was turned away. That’s when I got on one knee and tapped her on the shoulder.”

And although the “Toro” singer admitted that he was so nervous he “could feel my heart pounding,” he was able to pull off the surprise, which Miley shared wasn’t a simple feat.

“I am not easy to surprise,” she admitted during an appearance on Good Morning America following her engagement news. “I love to control every situation. I had completely surrendered. I’m telling you, I was so, so, so surprised.”

And before long, she was teasing that her and Maxx’s special day was on the horizon, releasing “Let’s Get Married” on her September 2026 album Bass Persuades, with the pair also giving an intimate glimpse into their romance in the song's music video.

“Let’s go get married tonight / Let’s get married / I’m having visions of white,” Miley sings in the track's chorus. “Let’s get married / Let’s turn the rest of our lives to a holiday / Anytime, any place, we can run away / Get married tonight / Let’s get married.”

But the couple didn't only work together on the romantic track, as Maxx additionally produced several other songs on the album.

And there’s a reason they always find their way back home to the studio, as Miley told Harper’s Bazaar in 2024, “Maxx just inspires me so much.”

But beyond their musical collaborations, the “Flowers” singer also credits Maxx with helping her with “starting over” and “building again” after she and ex Liam's California home burned down in the 2018 Malibu fires, before later separating.

“After I lost my house in the fires, that wasn't all that I lost,” Miley shared during a December press conference, per Hello! magazine. “I really actually went down to the ashes and rebuilt my life personally and physically [with] my home.”

And as Miley and Maxx enter the new chapter of their romance as newlyweds, keep reading for a look back at where their relationship began…

Miley Cyrus and musician Maxx Morando began dating in 2021. "We got put on a blind date," she recalled to British Vogue in 2023. "Well, it was blind for me and not really for him. I thought, 'The worst that can happen is I leave.'"

Miley didn't leave and, as their romance blossomed, the pair went red carpet official at the Gucci Love Parade event on November 2, 2021.

Maxx was by Miley's side when she won her first-ever Grammy at the 2024 award ceremony. The couple even shared a sweet smooch at their table during the event, and Miley referred to Maxx in her acceptance speech as "my love."

They returned to the Grammy Awards in 2025 for another public date night. "He’s very similar to me," Miley told Harper's Bazaar in an interview published November 2024. "We just don’t take life too seriously."

The musicians got glammed up for a big night out at the 2025 Oscars ceremony.

And later that night they stepped out in fresh looks for an appearance at the Vanity Fair Oscar Party.

It was a party in the U.S.A. when the pair got married in Plam Springs.

'SNL' Cast Members Confirm the Brutal Reality That You Don’t See Behind the Camera

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Greer Riddell is a London-based film and television journalist writing about comedy, streaming and the evolving media industry in the UK and USA. She works at Collider, covering the latest in film, television and entertainment through interviews, features, reviews and industry coverage.

Her recent brand collaborations include HBO, Warner Bros., Netflix, Hulu, Sky, Disney+ and DC Studios. She has covered titles including Saturday Night Live UK, Rivals, Scrubs, Stuart Fails to Save the Universe, Ludwig, Supergirl and Stranger Things.

Her recent interviews include Florence Pugh, Mike Faist and Christopher Abbott for Netflix's East of Eden, Taika Waititi and Jenna Ortega for Klara and the Sun.

Saturday Night Live UK writes around 35 sketches every week, but only six make it to the live show. Roughly 83% of the comedy created for an episode never reaches television, and even a sketch that makes it through rehearsals can be cut just 90 minutes before broadcast. The cast might already be in costume, the host might have performed it in front of a live audience, and the joke might have landed, but none of that guarantees it a place in the final show. If another sketch gets bigger laughs or makes for a stronger episode, the first one is out.

That ruthless selection process is central to what makes Saturday Night Live work. The show has to produce an hour of live television every week, which means there is little time to rescue a sketch that is not working or make room for one because its writers have worked hard on it. At the Edinburgh TV Festival 2026, SNL UK cast members Emma Sidi and Jack Shep, alongside Lead Producer James Longman discussed what happens to the comedy viewers never see. The result is a format in which the audience does more than watch the show — their laughter helps decide which version of the episode ultimately makes it to air.

Every Wednesday, the SNL UK team perform around 30 to 35 sketches at the table read before selecting the six that will move forward. Longman described the process as having a certain “purity” because hearing the scripts together gives the team an immediate sense of what is funny and what is not quite working. There is no guarantee that a sketch will survive because its premise sounds promising on paper — it has to compete with everything else written that week.

For the writers, that means rejection is not an occasional setback but a routine part of the job. Co-head writer Jonno Johnson explained that with more than 30 scripts to choose from, plenty of writers will inevitably find themselves without a sketch in the show. The challenge, he said, is learning “how you handle kind of failure and rejection.” That is a particularly important skill in a format that moves so quickly. Writers have only a few days to develop material, and once the team has chosen its sketches, there is little opportunity to dwell on the ideas that did not make it so resilience is key.

Cast member Jack Shep admitted at the Edinburgh TV Festival that Wednesdays can be difficult precisely because so much of the material being discarded is funny. “You do like, you know, hours and hours of hilarious stuff, and you just have to let it go,” he said. One sketch he was sad to see get cut involved a group of friends trying to persuade someone not to leave her boyfriend because he worked at Jo Malone, and they would lose access to his staff discount! Oh, and the boyfriend was a “horrific blob.”

The UK desk isn’t chasing shock value — it’s using comedy to cut through the language everyone else tiptoes around.

At 6pm on Saturday, SNL UK stages a dress rehearsal in front of a live audience, running through a longer version of the show before the 10pm broadcast. For the first time, the team can see how the sketches work as performances in front of a crowd. Longman explained that this is when they begin to feel the energy of the show, discover which jokes get laughs, and which do not. The audience's reaction becomes part of the editing process, helping the team decide what deserves to stay.

Even then, getting a laugh is no guarantee of making it to air. Emma Sidi recalled at the Edinburgh TV Festival a sketch cut after the dress rehearsal for the second episode of Season 1, hosted by Jamie Dornan. The segment was "Loose Men", a Loose Women/The View-style talk show in which Dornan blew bubbles. It had gone well in front of the audience, but Sidi admitted, “All the other sketches were way better.”

This is where the live audience becomes more than a group of people attending to purely enjoy a comedy show. Director Liz Clare explained at the Edinburgh TV Festival that the audience in the room takes precedence over home viewers because their laughter helps fuel the performance. The cast is effectively playing to two audiences — the people watching in the room and the viewers who will see the live broadcast at home. A laugh in the room gives the team useful information about the sketch, while the final decision depends on how the material fits into the episode as a whole. The audience are therefore unknowingly helping shape what millions of viewers eventually see.

After the dress rehearsal, Longman and the team gather to cut two sketches. With around 90 minutes left before the broadcast, the running order can be rearranged, material rewritten, and entire sections removed. “Everything gets scrambled, rearranged,” Longman explained. “It keeps getting rewritten, and then the show goes out of 10.” By then, a sketch can have survived the writing process, made it into rehearsal, and been performed with the host already in costume, only to disappear before the audience at home ever gets to see it. That is the brutal reality of the SNL format!

Even those at the top are not immune. Johnson recalled writing a sketch about a planet where everyone was called Paul Selby, and its inhabitants could not understand how Earth society functioned with everyone having different names. The premise seemed perfectly reasonable while he was writing it, but watching it back left him questioning the idea and his sanity!

Johnson admitted that the writers who do best on SNL UK are those willing to play with other people's ideas and abandon their own when needs must. There is not enough time to keep refining a sketch that is not working. Sidi expressed a similar attitude, explaining that writers have to be able to recognize that an idea was good and still accept that it is dead. As Longman put it, “It's all about the show. It's not about what I think is funny.”

That is the logic behind Saturday Night Live's 17% rule. SNL UK is not trying to preserve every promising idea or reward the people who have spent the longest working on it. It is trying to make the strongest possible show from the material available, using the audience's reaction as one of the tools that helps it get there. For viewers at home, the result is a show that appears to move effortlessly from one sketch to the next. Behind that apparent ease is a process of constant comparison and rejection, with most of the comedy created each week left on the cutting-room floor. Let's hope for an extended cut one day.

Saturday Night Live UK Season 2 Part 2 returns on Sky One in the UK in January 2027 and streams the next day on Peacock in the U.S.
